Unifying Linearity and Dependency Analyses Journal: PACMPL CCS: Theory of computation Linear logic CCS: Theory of computation Type theory CCS: Security and privacy Formal security models
Pritam Choudhury

Abstract
. Linearity and dependency analyses are key to several applications in computer science, especially, in resource management and information flow control. What connects these analyses is that both of them need to model at least two different worlds with constrained mutual interaction. To elaborate, a typical linearity analysis would model nonlinear and linear worlds with the constraint that derivations in the nonlinear world cannot make use of assumptions from the linear world; a typical dependency analysis would model low-security and high-security worlds with the constraint that information from the high-security world never leaks into the low-security world. Now, though linearity and dependency analyses address similar problems, these analyses are carried out by employing different methods. For linearity analysis, type systems employ the comonadic exponential modality from Girard’s linear logic. For dependency analysis, type systems employ the monadic modality from Moggi’s computational metalanguage. Owing to this methodical difference, a unification of the two analyses, though theoretically and practically desirable, is not straightforward. Fortunately, with recent advances in graded-context type systems, it has been realized that linearity and dependency analyses can be viewed through the same lens. However, existing graded-context type systems fall short of a unification of linearity and dependency analyses. The problem with existing graded-context type systems is that though their linearity analysis is general, their dependency analysis is limited, primarily because the graded modality they employ is comonadic and not monadic. In this paper, we address this limitation by systematically extending existing graded-context type systems so that the graded modality is both comonadic and monadic. This extension enables us to unify linearity analysis with a general dependency analysis. We present a unified Linear Dependency Calculus, LDC, which analyses linearity and dependency using the same mechanism in an arbitrary Pure Type System. We show that LDC is a general linear and dependency calculus by subsuming into it the standard calculi for the individual analyses.



1.
Introduction

Type systems formalize our intuition of correct programs: upon formalization, a correct program is one that is well-typed. Our intuition of correctness, however, varies depending upon application. For example: in a distributed system, correctness would entail absence of simultaneous write access to a file by multiple users; in a security system, correctness would entail absence of read access to secret files by public users. To formalize such and other similar notions of correctness, we employ linear type systems and dependency type systems. These type systems have a wide variety of applications.

Linear type systems are good at
managing resource usage
. They can reason about state and enable compiler optimizations like in-place update of memory locations
(
Wadler 1990
)
. They can be employed for fine-grained memory management
(
Chirimar
et al. 2000
;
Fluet
et al. 2006
)
. Through session types, they can guarantee absence of deadlocks in distributed systems
(
Caires
et al. 2016
)
. Owing to their utility, linear types have found their way into several programming languages, like Haskell
(
Bernardy et al. 2018
)
, Granule
(
Orchard
et al. 2019
)
, Idris 2
(
Brady 2021
)
.

Dependency type systems are good at
controlling information flow
. In the form of security type systems
(
Heintze and
Riecke 1998
;
Volpano
et al. 1996
)
, they guarantee that low-security outputs do not depend upon high-security inputs. In the form of binding-time type systems
(
Gomard and Jones 1991
;
Davies 2017
)
, they guarantee that early-bound expressions do not depend upon late-bound ones. Dependency type systems are quite common in practical programming languages. For example, the metaprogramming language MetaOcaml
(
Calcagno
et al. 2003
)
is based on the dependency type system of
Davies 2017
. The Jif extension of Java, employed for ensuring secure information flow, is based on the dependency type system of
Myers 1999
.

Though linear type systems and dependency type systems serve different purposes, they essentially address the same abstract problem. The problem is to model at least two different worlds that interact following given constraints. For example: a typical linear type system needs to model nonlinear and linear worlds with the constraint that derivations in the nonlinear world cannot make use of assumptions from the linear world; a typical dependency type system needs to model low-security and high-security worlds with the constraint that information from the high-security world never leaks into the low-security world. This fundamental similarity suggests that linearity and dependency analyses could be unified.

There are several benefits to such a unification. First, from a theoretical perspective, it would unify the standard calculi for linearity analysis
(
Benton 1994
;
Barber 1996
)
with the standard calculi for dependency analysis
(
Abadi
et al. 1999
;
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
)
. Second, from a practical perspective, it would allow programmers to use the same type system for both linearity and dependency analyses. Presently, programmers use different type systems for this purpose. For example, Haskell programmers use the Linear Haskell extension
(
Bernardy et al. 2018
)
for linearity analysis and LIO library
(
Stefan et al. 2017
)
for dependency analysis. Third, it would allow a combination of the two analyses. A combined analysis is more powerful than the individual analyses done separately because it would allow arbitrary combination of usage and flow constraints. For example, in a combined analysis, a piece of data may be simultaneously linear and private.

A unification of linear and dependency type systems, though desirable, is not straightforward. This is so because these type systems employ different methods to enforce their respective constraints. Linear type systems
(
Abramsky 1993
;
Benton
et al. 1993
;
Barber 1996
;
Turner and Wadler 1999
)
employ the
comonadic
exponential modality,
!
, taken from linear logic
(
Girard 1987
)
, to manipulate nonlinear resources in a base linear world. On the other hand, dependency type systems
(
Abadi
et al. 1999
;
Heintze and
Riecke 1998
;
Volpano
et al. 1996
)
employ the
monadic
modality,
T
, taken from computational metalanguage
(
Moggi 1991
)
, to manipulate high-security values in a base low-security world.

The modalities
!
and
T
behave differently. For example: In a dependency calculus, there is no non-constant function of type
T\>\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool}
because any such function would leak information. However, in a linear calculus, there exists a non-constant function,
\emptyset\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{derelict}\>\mathit{x}:\>!\>\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool}
, since nonlinear resources can be used linearly. Next, in a dependency calculus,
\mathit{x}:\mathbf{Bool}\vdash\eta\>\mathit{x}:\>T\>\mathbf{Bool}
is a valid typing judgment because information can flow from low-security world to high-security world. However, in a linear calculus,
\mathit{x}:\mathbf{Bool}\vdash!\>\mathit{x}:\>!\>\mathbf{Bool}
is not a valid typing judgment because nonlinear resources cannot make use of linear assumptions. Owing to these differences, for a long time, linearity and dependency analyses have been carried out independent of one another.

However, with recent advances in graded-context type systems
(
Ghica and Smith 2014
;
Brunel
et al. 2014
;
Petricek
et al. 2014
;
Orchard
et al. 2019
;
Abel and Bernardy 2020
)
, it has been realized that the two analyses can be viewed through the same lens. Graded-context type systems are type systems parametrized over preordered semirings or similar abstract algebraic structures. These type systems can carry out a wide variety of usage analyses through different instantiations of the parametrizing structure. In other words, they are not limited to linear/nonlinear usage analysis but can also analyze other forms of usage, like no usage, affine usage, bounded usage, etc. As such, graded-context type systems are more general than linear type systems. Now, to understand how a graded-context type system might analyze dependency, we need to consider the similarity between the modalities
!
and
T
, from the perspective of usage analysis. Assuming the base world to be linear, the monadic modality,
T
, may be understood as indicating no usage because terms from a ‘monad world’ cannot be used outside that world. This understanding brings the modalities
!
(indicating unrestricted usage) and
T
(indicating no usage) under the same umbrella and gives us a perspective on the differences in their behavior mentioned above.

However, the problem with existing graded-context type systems is that though their linearity analysis is general, their dependency analysis is limited. There are several aspects of dependency analysis that these systems cannot capture. We discuss them in detail in the next section. The main reason behind this shortcoming is that graded-context type systems have been designed for analyzing coeffects, i.e. how programs depend upon their contexts. Coeffects include linearity (single usage), irrelevance (no usage), etc. Dependency, however, behaves more like an effect. To elaborate: low and high security computations may be seen as pure and effectful computations respectively. An effect like dependency is not well captured by existing graded-context type systems, which are basically coeffect calculi.

In this paper, we show that by systematically extending existing graded-context type systems, we can use them for a general dependency and linearity analysis. We design a calculus, LDC, that can simultaneously analyze, using the same mechanism, a coeffect like linearity and an effect like dependency. LDC is parametrized by an arbitrary Pure Type System and it subsumes standard calculi for linearity and dependency analyses. We show that linearity and dependency analyses in LDC are correct using a heap semantics.

In summary, we make the following contributions:

•

We present a language, LDC, parametrized by an arbitrary pure type system, that analyzes linearity and dependency using the same mechanism.

•

We show that LDC subsumes the standard calculi for analyzing linearity and dependency, for example, Linear Nonlinear
\lambda
-calculus of
Benton 1994
, DCC of
Abadi
et al. 1999
, Sealing Calculus of
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
, etc.

•

We show that correctness of both linearity and dependency analyses in LDC follow from the soundness theorem for the calculus.

•

We show that LDC can carry out a combined linearity and dependency analysis.

2.
Challenges and Resolution

2.1.
Dependency Analysis: Salient Aspects

In the previous section, we discussed about dependency analysis with respect to low-security and high-security worlds. Such an analysis can be extended to an arbitrary (finite) number of worlds with dependency constraints among them.
Denning 1976
observed that dependency constraints upon worlds result in a lattice structure. Recall that a lattice
\mathcal{L}=(L,\sqsubseteq)
is a partially-ordered set, where every pair of elements has a least upper bound, also called join and denoted by
\sqcup
, and a greatest lower bound, also called meet and denoted by
\sqcap
. If
L
is finite, then it has a top element and a bottom element, denoted
\top
and
\bot
respectively, such that
\bot\sqsubseteq\ell\sqsubseteq\top
, for all
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
. Now, to give an example of a dependency lattice, consider the following set of worlds: a low-security world
\mathbf{L}
, two medium-security worlds
\mathbf{M_{1}}
and
\mathbf{M_{2}}
that do not share information with each other, and a high-security world
\mathbf{H}
. These constraints may be modeled by a diamond lattice,
\mathcal{L}_{\diamond}
, where
\mathbf{L}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{M_{1}}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{L}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{M_{2}}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{H}
, with the idea that any information flow that goes against this lattice order is illegal. More generally, for an arbitrary lattice,
\mathcal{L}
, given levels
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\in\mathcal{L}
, we have:
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
if and only if information may flow from
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
to
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Dependency type systems
(
Abadi
et al. 1999
;
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
)
are based on this lattice model of information flow. These type systems grade the monadic modality,
T
, of Moggi’s computational metalanguage with labels drawn from an abstract dependency lattice,
\mathcal{L}
. The idea behind the grading is that for any
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
,
T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
would denote
\ell
-secure terms of type
\mathit{A}
. Figuratively,
T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
represents the terms of type
\mathit{A}
, but enclosed in
\ell
-secure boxes, which may be opened only with
\ell
-security clearance. For example,
T_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathbf{Bool}
is the type of high-secure booleans, which may be observed only with high-security clearance. Now, a dependency type system may be said to be sound if it ensures that
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
-secure inputs do not affect
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
-secure outputs, whenever
\neg(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
. This principle may be rephrased as: whenever
\neg(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
, one cannot gain any information about inputs from
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
by observing outputs at
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. This is the well-known principle of
noninterference
, from which dependency type systems draw their strength. To give a concrete example, a corollary of this principle would be that given security lattice
\mathcal{L}_{\diamond}
, any function of type
T_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathbf{Bool}\to T_{\mathbf{L}}\>\mathbf{Bool}
is a constant function.

Now, we consider the nature of the graded modality,
T_{\ell}
. While analyzing dependency, one should be able to move freely between the types
T_{\ell}\>T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
and
T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
because enclosing a term in a cascade of
\ell
-secure boxes is the same as enclosing it in a single
\ell
-secure box. Moving from
T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
to
T_{\ell}\>T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
is easy because one just needs to put an extra wrapper. But moving from
T_{\ell}\>T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
to
T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
requires some consideration because it involves unwrapping. To handle the situation, one can invoke the properties of the modality. The modality, being monadic, supports the standard join operation:
T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}
, where
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\in\mathcal{L}
. Via this join operation, one can move from
T_{\ell}\>T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
to
T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
. Now, if dependency labels correspond to effects, then the join operation corresponds to computing the union of these effects. Just as computing union is important in an effect calculus, having a join operation is important in a dependency calculus. Later in this section, we shall see that existing graded-context type systems cannot derive a join operation. This significantly limits dependency analysis in these type systems.

Before moving further, we want to point out another important aspect of dependency analysis that is sometimes ignored. It pertains to the treatment of functions that are wrapped under
T_{\mathbf{H}}
. Consider the type:
T_{\mathbf{H}}\>(T_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool})
. What should the values of this type be? DCC
(
Abadi
et al. 1999
)
would answer: just
\eta_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{true})
and
\eta_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{false})
. But Sealing Calculus
(
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
)
would answer:
\mathbf{seal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{true})
,
\mathbf{seal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{false})
,
\mathbf{seal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{unseal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathit{x})
and
\mathbf{seal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{not}\>(\mathbf{unseal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathit{x}))
. This difference stems from the fact that in Sealing Calculus, the function
T_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool}
, if wrapped under
T_{\mathbf{H}}
, may return a high-security output whereas in DCC, it must always return a constant output. In this regard, Sealing Calculus is more general than DCC because it doesn’t restrict any function from returning high-security values, if the function itself is wrapped under the high-security label,
\mathbf{H}
. Note here that over terminating computations, Sealing Calculus subsumes DCC and is, in fact, more general than DCC, as we see above.

What makes Sealing Calculus more general than DCC is the form of its typing judgment. While DCC employs the traditional form of typing judgment, Sealing Calculus employs a labeled typing judgment of the form:

(1)

\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}\vdash b:^{\ell}B,

where
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
. The intuitive meaning of this judgment is that
b
is an
\ell
-secure term of type
\mathit{B}
, assuming
x_{i}
has type
A_{i}
, for
i=1,2,\ldots,n
. The key advantage of a labeled typing judgment is that it facilitates smooth sealing and unsealing of secure values, as we see in the rules below:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{seal}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\>\mathit{a}:^{\ell}T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\>\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:SC-Seal}{{{SC-Seal}}}
}{},}

\mpr@andcr
\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\>\mathit{A}\hskip 8.19447pt\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}\sqsubseteq\ell}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{unseal}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\>\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:SC-Unseal}{{{SC-Unseal}}}
}{},}

On the other hand, DCC needs to employ a nonstandard
bind
-rule along with an auxiliary protection judgment in order to unseal secure values. The labeled typing judgment of Sealing Calculus also enables any function to return high-security outputs, provided the function itself is wrapped under a high-security label, as discussed above. Owing to these good properties, we use the Sealing Calculus as our model for dependency analysis. As an aside, we allow nonterminating computations in our language, even though Sealing Calculus does not. For a more elaborate comparison of Sealing Calculus and DCC, see
Choudhury 2022
.

2.2.
Graded-Context Type Systems: Salient Aspects

Over the recent years, graded-context type systems
(
Ghica and Smith 2014
;
Brunel
et al. 2014
;
Petricek
et al. 2014
;
Gaboardi et al. 2016
;
McBride 2016
;
Atkey 2018
;
Orchard
et al. 2019
;
Abel and Bernardy 2020
;
Choudhury et al. 2021
;
Moon
et al. 2021
)
have been successfully employed for reasoning about resource usage in programs. Graded-context type systems have their roots in bounded linear logic
(
Girard
et al. 1992
)
, which adapts the ‘propositions as resources’ doctrine of linear logic to characterize resource-bound computations. To achieve its goal, bounded linear logic imposes wider distinctions on usage of resources, compared to linear logic. Graded-context type systems draw inspiration from bounded linear logic in widening the nature and scope of usage analysis, compared to traditional linear type systems
(
Benton 1994
;
Barber 1996
)
.

The power and flexibility of graded-context type systems stem from the fact that they are parametrized by an abstract preordered semiring or a similar structure that represents an algebra of resources. Recall that a semiring,
(Q,+,\cdot,0,1)
, is a set
Q
with two binary operators,
+
(addition) and
\cdot
(multiplication), along with their respective identities,
0
and
1
, such that addition is commutative and associative, multiplication is associative and distributive over addition, and
0
is an annihilator for multiplication. A preordered semiring
\mathcal{Q}=(Q,+,\cdot,0,1,<:)
is a semiring
(Q,+,\cdot,0,1)
with a binary preorder relation,
<:
, that respects the binary operations, meaning, if
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}<:\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, then
\mathit{q}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}<:\mathit{q}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}<:\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}
, for all
q\in Q
. By varying the parameter
\mathcal{Q}
, graded-context type systems can carry out a variety of analyses. Among these analyses, two specific ones that interest us are linearity and affinity. The preordered semirings employed for the two analyses, denoted
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
respectively, both have 3 elements:
0,1
and
\omega
, with
1+1=\omega+1=1+\omega=\omega+\omega=\omega
and
\omega\cdot\omega=\omega
. However,
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
are ordered differently, as shown in Figure
1
. Owing to this difference in ordering,
1
signifies linear usage in
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
but affine usage in
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
. However, in both
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
,
0
signifies no usage while
\omega
signifies unrestricted usage.

\omega
1
0

(a)

Ordering in
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}

\omega
1
0

(b)

Ordering in
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}

Figure 1
.
Ordering for tracking linear and affine use

Graded-context type systems use elements of the parametrizing preordered semiring,
\mathcal{Q}
, to grade the
!
-modality. The idea behind the grading is that for any
\mathit{q}\in\mathcal{Q}
,
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
would denote
\mathit{q}
-usage terms of type
\mathit{A}
. For example, when
\mathcal{Q}
is set to
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
, the type
\mspace{2mu}!_{1}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
represents terms of type
A
that must be used exactly once. Again, when
\mathcal{Q}
is set to
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
, the type
\mspace{2mu}!_{1}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
represents terms that may be used at most once. However in both the cases, the type
\mspace{2mu}!_{\omega}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
represents terms that may be used without restriction. Now, a graded-context type system may be said to be sound if it accounts usage correctly, thereby ensuring fairness of usage. Fairness of usage implies absence of arbitrary copying or discarding of resources. To give a concrete example, in a graded-context polymorphic type system, parametrized by
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
, the types
\forall\alpha.\mspace{2mu}!_{1}\mspace{1mu}\alpha\to\mspace{2mu}!_{1}\mspace{1mu}\alpha\>\times\>\mspace{2mu}!_{1}\mspace{1mu}\alpha
and
\forall\alpha.\mspace{2mu}!_{1}\mspace{1mu}\alpha\to\mathbf{Unit}
should be uninhabited.

The key feature of graded-context type systems is that they grade contexts of typing judgments with elements of the parametrizing structure. A typical typing judgment in a graded-context type system, parametrized by a preordered semiring,
\mathcal{Q}
, looks like:

(2)

\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ldots,x_{n}:^{q_{n}}A_{n}\vdash b:B,

where
q_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}
. Note that a graded context,
\Gamma
, can be decomposed into a ungraded context,
\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor
, and a vector of grades,
\overline{\Gamma}
. The intuitive meaning of the graded context
x_{1}:^{q_{1}}A_{1},x_{2}:^{q_{2}}A_{2},\ldots,x_{n}:^{q_{n}}A_{n}
is the same as that of the standard context
x_{1}:\,!_{q_{1}}A_{1},x_{2}:\,!_{q_{2}}A_{2},\ldots,x_{n}:\,!_{q_{n}}A_{n}
. However, there is a very good reason behind employing graded contexts in place of standard ones: context operations can be easily defined on graded contexts by lifting the corresponding operations of the preordered semiring to the level of contexts. For example, for graded contexts
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, given
\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor
, the context addition operation,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, is defined by pointwise addition of grades and the context multiplication operation,
q\cdot\Gamma
, is defined by pre-multiplying every grade in
\Gamma
by
\mathit{q}
. These context operations facilitate smooth accounting of resource usage in graded-context type systems. The introduction and elimination rules for
!_{q}
, shown below, illustrates how.

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}}{\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{a}:\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:GC-ExpIntro}{{{GC-ExpIntro}}}
}{},}

\mpr@andcr
\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:\mathit{B}\hskip 8.19447pt\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}\>!_{\mathit{q}}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:GC-ExpElim}{{{GC-ExpElim}}}
}{},}

Next, we consider the nature of the graded modality,
!_{q}
. This graded modality is graded comonadic over
(Q,\cdot,1)

(
Fujii 2019
;
Katsumata 2018
)
, meaning, one can derive the standard extract and fork functions for this modality:

\displaystyle\mathbf{extract}:=\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{let}\>!_{1}\>\mathit{y}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{y}\;:\>\mspace{2mu}!_{1}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}

\displaystyle\mathbf{fork}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}:=\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{let}\>!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mathit{y}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{y}\;:\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}\to\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}

Here,
\mathit{A}
is an arbitrary type and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\in Q
. The comonadic nature of this modality is essential to supporting usage analysis. To give an example, for the promotion
\mspace{2mu}!_{\omega}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}\to\mspace{2mu}!_{\omega}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\omega}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
, one needs
\mathbf{fork}^{\omega,\omega}
. Though the fork function is necessary for usage analysis, the same is not true of the corresponding join function. As such, graded-context type systems do not derive a join function. In fact, these type systems
cannot
derive a join function in general, meaning, there is no general function of type
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}\to\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
derivable in these type systems. Proposition
A.1
in Appendix
A
presents a model-theoretic proof of this claim. This shortcoming of graded-context type systems, while inconsequential for usage analysis, is limiting for dependency analysis, as we see next.

2.3.
Limitations of Dependency Analysis in Graded-Context Type Systems

Existing graded-context type systems are limited in their analysis of dependency. Below, we elaborate why.

(1)

Graded-context type systems are parametrized by preordered semirings. Dependency analysis, however, is parametrized by lattices. Both preordered semirings and lattices are algebraic structures with two binary operators and a binary order relation. However, a crucial distinction between these structures is that while semirings need to ensure that one operator (multiplication) distributes over the other (addition), lattices do not need to ensure any such property. In fact, there are lattices where neither operator distributes over the other. The simplest of such lattices are
M_{3}
and
N_{5}

(
Birkhoff 1967
)
, both with 5 elements, ordered as shown in Figure
2
.

\bot
\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
\top

(a)

Lattice
M_{3}

\bot
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
\top

(b)

Lattice
N_{5}

Figure 2
.
Examples of non-distributive lattices

In
M_{3}
, join and meet do not distribute over one another:

\displaystyle(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})\>\sqcap\>(\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})=\top\neq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}})\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\displaystyle(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})\>\sqcup\>(\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})=\bot\neq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}})\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}.

The same is true of
N_{5}
:

\displaystyle(\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}})\>\sqcap\>(\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}})=\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}\neq\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}})\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\displaystyle(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}})\>\sqcup\>(\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}})=\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\neq\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}=(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}.

Thus, an arbitrary lattice can not be viewed as a preordered semiring. Distributive lattices, i.e. lattices where join and meet distribute over one another, however, may be viewed as preordered semirings where multiplication, addition and order are given by join, meet and the lattice order respectively. So, existing graded-context type systems could
potentially
carry out dependency analysis over distributive lattices. However, we view this as a limitation since we see no principled justification in restricting dependency analysis to distributive lattices only.

(2)

Dependency analysis needs a join operator, as discussed in Section
2.1
. However, graded-context type systems cannot derive a join operator, as discussed in Section
2.2
. An ad hoc solution to this problem might be to add an explicit join operator to the type system, as follows:

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mathit{a}:\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:GC-Join}{{{GC-Join}}}
}{},}

However, such a solution is not very satisfactory owing to the following reasons:

•

This
\mathbf{join}
operator raises problems in operational semantics, especially with call-by-name reduction. To understand why, consider the question: how should
\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mathit{b}
reduce in a call-by-name calculus? To reduce terms headed by
\mathbf{join}
, one might come up with the following rules:

\frac{\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}}{\vdash\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mathit{a^{\prime}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Step-JoinLeft}{{{Step-JoinLeft}}}
}{},}

\mpr@andcr
\frac{}{\vdash\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{a}\leadsto\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{a}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Step-JoinBeta}{{{Step-JoinBeta}}}
}{},}

Then, to reduce
\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mathit{b}
, one would first reduce
\mathit{b}
to a value and thereafter attempt to apply rule
Step-JoinBeta
. But notice that such an attempt might not be successful even for well-typed terms. This is so because in a call-by-name calculus,
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{a^{\prime}}
is a value, irrespective of whether
\mathit{a^{\prime}}
itself is a value or not. As such, the reduction of
\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mathit{b}
might just stop at a term like
\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{b^{\prime}}
, where
\mathit{b^{\prime}}
is not headed by
!_{q_{2}}
. In such a case, the term
\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{b^{\prime}}
cannot step via rule
Step-JoinBeta
. Given this situation, to maintain type soundness, one would be forced to declare terms like
\mathbf{join}^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{b^{\prime}}
as values, thereby allowing unprincipled values within the calculus.

•

Further, the addition of this
\mathbf{join}
operator breaks the symmetry of the type system since rule
GC-Join
is neither an introduction rule nor an elimination rule for
!
. Owing to this break in symmetry, there is no principled way to ensure that
\mathbf{join}
is the inverse of
\mathbf{fork}
, which is already derivable in the type system, as shown in Section
2.2
. Thus, this
\mathbf{join}
operator does not bode well for the equational theory of the type system as well.

In light of these drawbacks, we conclude that adding an ad hoc
\mathbf{join}
operator to enable dependency analysis in a graded-context type system is not the right approach. So, we avoid this approach.

(3)

Next, as discussed in Section
2.1
, we want a dependency calculus where any function can return high-security values, whenever the function itself is wrapped under a high-security label. Existing graded-context type systems do not allow this. For example, in a graded-context type system parametrized by the lattice,
\mathbf{L}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{H}
, the type
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathbf{H}}\mspace{1mu}(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathbf{H}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool})
contains only two distinct terms,
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathbf{H}}\mspace{1mu}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{true})
and
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathbf{H}}\mspace{1mu}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{false})
. Put differently, in a graded-context type system, there are no terms corresponding to
\mathbf{seal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{unseal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathit{x})
and
\mathbf{seal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>(\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{not}\>(\mathbf{unseal}_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathit{x}))
from the Sealing Calculus.

Hence, we see that there are several impediments to dependency analysis in existing graded-context type systems. This motivates us to look for other solutions for unifying linearity and dependency analyses.

2.4.
Towards Resolution

Recent work by
Choudhury
et al. 2022
points towards a possible way of unifying linearity and dependency analyses.
Choudhury
et al. 2022
present
\text{DDC}^{\top}
, a type system for general dependency analysis in Pure Type Systems.
\text{DDC}^{\top}
, though similar to existing graded-context type systems, avoids all their limitations listed above.
\text{DDC}^{\top}
is parametrized by an arbitrary lattice and subsumes the Sealing Calculus. Further,
\text{DDC}^{\top}
can analyze dependencies in a dependent setting, for example, run-time irrelevance in dependently-typed programs. The key difference between
\text{DDC}^{\top}
and graded-context type systems is in the form of their typing judgments.
\text{DDC}^{\top}
, in addition to grading contexts of typing judgments, also puts labels on the judgments, as shown below:

(3)

\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ldots,x_{n}:^{\ell_{n}}A_{n}\vdash b:^{\ell}B,

where
\ell_{i},\ell
are elements of the parametrizing lattice. The label to the right of the turnstile in (
3
) denotes the observer’s level. In graded-context type systems, the observer’s level is fixed at
1
. The added flexibility of varying the observer’s level enables
\text{DDC}^{\top}
carry out a general dependency analysis.

However,
\text{DDC}^{\top}
cannot carry out linearity analysis. So the problem of unifying linearity and dependency analyses still remains open. Nevertheless, we can take inspiration from
\text{DDC}^{\top}
and see what happens when we allow graded-context type systems to vary the observer’s level via typing judgments of the form:

(4)

\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ldots,x_{n}:^{q_{n}}A_{n}\vdash b:^{q}B,

where
q_{i},q\in\mathcal{Q}
. This typing judgment is essentially a fusion of the typing judgments of the Sealing Calculus and existing graded-context type systems, shown in (
1
) and (
2
) respectively. This seems to be a good start. However, a known roadblock awaits us along this way.
Atkey 2018
showed that a type system that uses the above judgment form and is parametrized by an
arbitrary
semiring does not admit substitution. This roadblock is discouraging but we found that it is not a dead end. We found that though substitution is inadmissible over some semirings, it is in fact admissible over several preordered semirings that interest us. In particular, substitution is admissible over the standard preordered semirings employed for tracking linearity and affinity, viz.,
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
respectively.

Thus, we finally have a way to unify linearity and dependency analyses. In our Linear Dependency Calculus, LDC, we use typing judgments of the form shown in (
4
). For linearity and other usage analyses, we parametrize LDC over certain preordered semirings that we describe as we go along. For dependency analysis, we parametrize LDC over an
arbitrary
lattice. For a combined linearity and dependency analysis, we parametrize LDC over the cartesian product of the structures used for the individual analyses.

Now, LDC can analyze linearity and dependency in an arbitrary pure type system. However, some of the key ideas of the calculus are best explained in a simply-typed setting. So, we first present the simply-typed version of LDC and thereafter generalize it to its pure type system version.

3.
Linearity and Dependency Analyses in Simple Type Systems

3.1.
Type System for Linearity Analysis

First, we shall analyze exact usage and bounded usage. We shall add unrestricted usage, referred to by
\omega
, to our calculus in Section
6
. Exact usage can be analyzed by
\mathbb{N}_{=}
, the semiring of natural numbers with discrete order. Bounded usage can be analyzed by
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
, the semiring of natural numbers with descending natural order. The ordering in
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
looks like:
\ldots<:4<:3<:2<:1<:0
. The reason behind the difference in ordering is that in bounded usage analysis, resources may be discarded. But note that resources are never copied in either of these analyses.

Next, we present LDC parametrized over these two preordered semirings. Whenever we need precision, we refer to LDC parametrized over an algebraic structure,
\mathcal{AS}
, as LDC(
\mathcal{AS}
). The algebraic structure,
\mathcal{AS}
, may be either a preordered semiring or a lattice or their cartesian product (in case of combined analysis).

Now, let
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
vary over
\{\mathbb{N}_{=},\mathbb{N}_{\geq}\}
. The grammar of LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
) appears in Figure
3
. The calculus has function types, product types, sum types and a
\mathbf{Unit}
type. Product types correspond to multiplicative conjunction of linear logic; sum type corresponds to additive disjunction. Observe that the function type and the product type are annotated with grades. Types
{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
are essentially
(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{r}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A})\to\mathit{B}
and
(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{r}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
respectively. In lieu of
{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, we could have used unannotated function and product types, i.e.,
\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
respectively, along with a graded exponential modal type,
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{r}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
. However, we chose to present this way because it generalizes easily to the Pure Type System setting. For terms, we have the introduction and the elimination forms corresponding to these types. The terms are also annotated with grades that track resources used by them. Assumptions in the context also appear along with their allowed usages.

\displaystyle\text{types},A,B,C

\displaystyle::=\mathbf{Unit}\>|\>{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\>|\>{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>|\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\text{terms},a,b,c

\displaystyle::=x\>|\>\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}\>|\>\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}\>

\displaystyle|\>\mathbf{unit}\>|\>\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}\>|\>(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\>|\>\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}

\displaystyle|\>\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>|\>\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>|\>\mathbf{case}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\text{contexts},\Gamma

\displaystyle::=\emptyset\>|\>\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}

Figure 3
.
Grammar of LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
) (Simple Version)

Next, we look at the type system. The type system appears in Figure
4
. There are a few things to note:

•

A graded context
\Gamma
is essentially a combination of the underlying ungraded context, denoted
\Delta:=\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor
, and the associated vector of grades, denoted
\overline{\Gamma}
.

•

For contexts
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor=\Delta
, we define
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
as:
\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta
and
\overline{\Gamma}=\overline{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\overline{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
(pointwise vector addition).

•

For context
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}
and grade
\mathit{q}
, we define
\Gamma:=\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}
as:
\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}\rfloor
and
\overline{\Gamma}=\mathit{q}\cdot\overline{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}}
(scalar multiplication).

•

For contexts
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma^{\prime}\rfloor
, we say
\Gamma<:\Gamma^{\prime}
if and only if
\overline{\Gamma}<:\overline{\Gamma^{\prime}}
(pointwise order).

•

For any context
\Gamma
, we implicitly assume that no two assumptions assign types to the same variable.

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}

(Simple Version)

\frac{}{0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A},0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Var}{{{ST-Var}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Lam}{{{ST-Lam}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-App}{{{ST-App}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Pair}{{{ST-Pair}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\quad\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-LetPair}{{{ST-LetPair}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{}{0\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{unit}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathbf{Unit}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Unit}{{{ST-Unit}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathbf{Unit}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\quad\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-LetUnit}{{{ST-LetUnit}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Inj1}{{{ST-Inj1}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Inj2}{{{ST-Inj2}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\quad\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{case}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Case}{{{ST-Case}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\quad\Gamma<:\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-SubL}{{{ST-SubL}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\quad\mathit{q}<:\mathit{q^{\prime}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q^{\prime}}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-SubR}{{{ST-SubR}}}
}{},}

Figure 4
.
Typing rules for LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
)

The typing judgment
\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ldots,x_{n}:^{q_{n}}A_{n}\vdash a:^{q}A
may be intuitively understood as: one can produce
\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{a}
of type
\mathit{A}
, using
q_{i}
copies of
x_{i}
of type
A_{i}
, where
i=1,2,\ldots,n
. With this understanding, let us look at the typing rules.

Most of the rules are as expected. A point to note is that the elimination rules
ST-LetPair
,
ST-LetUnit
, and
ST-Case
have a side condition
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1
. The reason behind this condition is that for reducing any of these elimination forms, we first need to reduce the term
\mathit{a}
, implying that in any case, we would need the resources for reducing at least one copy of
\mathit{a}
. We may set
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}
to
1
but allowing any
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1
makes these rules more flexible. For example, owing to this flexibility, in rule
ST-LetPair
,
\mathit{b}
may use
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}
copies of
\mathit{y}
in lieu of just
\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{y}
. Another point to note is how the rules
ST-SubL
and
ST-SubR
help discard resources in LDC(
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
).

Now, let us look at a few examples of derivable and non-derivable terms in LDC(
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
). (For the sake of simplicity, we omit the domain types in the
\lambda
s.)

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}:^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}\text{ but }\emptyset\nvdash\lambda^{0}\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}:^{1}{}^{0}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}:^{2}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}\text{ but }\emptyset\nvdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}.(\mathit{x}^{2},\mathbf{unit}):^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to{}^{2}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathbf{Unit}

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}.\mathbf{let}_{1}\>(\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{1},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{1}{}^{1}\!({}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A})\to\mathit{A}\text{ but }\emptyset\nvdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}.(\mathit{x}^{1},\mathit{x}):^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}

On a closer look, we find that the non-derivable terms above can not use resources fairly. Consider the types of these terms:
{}^{0}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
,
{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to{}^{2}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathbf{Unit}
and
{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}
. Such types may be inhabited only if resources can be copied. Since we disallow copying, they are essentially uninhabited. However, since LDC(
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
) allows discarding of resources, the type
{}^{1}\!({}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A})\to\mathit{A}
is, in fact, inhabited.

Note that in order to produce
0
copies of any term, we do not need any resources. So in the
0
world, any annotated type is inhabited, provided its unannotated counterpart is inhabited. However, resources do not have any meaning in the
0
world. In other words, the judgment
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{0}\mathit{A}
conveys no more information than its corresponding standard
\lambda
-calculus counterpart.

Next, we look at the operational semantics and metatheory of LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
).

3.2.
Metatheory of Linearity Analysis

First, we consider some syntactic properties. The calculus satisfies the multiplication lemma stated below. This lemma says that if we need
\Gamma
resources to produce
\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{a}
, then we would need
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma
resources to produce
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{a}
.

Lemma 3.1 (Multiplication).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
, then
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Note that we don’t provide proofs of the lemmas and the theorems in the main body of the paper but the proofs are available in the appendices.

The calculus also satisfies a factorization lemma, stated below. This lemma says that if a context
\Gamma
produces
\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{a}
, then
\Gamma
can be split into
\mathit{q}
parts whereby each part produces
1
copy of
\mathit{a}
. We need the precondition,
q\neq 0
, since resources don’t have any meaning in the
0
world but they are meaningful in all other worlds, in particular, the
1
world.

Lemma 3.2 (Factorization).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
q\neq 0
, then there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Using the above two lemmas, we can prove a splitting lemma, stated below. This lemma says that if we have the resources,
\Gamma
, to produce
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
copies of
\mathit{a}
, then we can split
\Gamma
into two parts,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, such that
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
can produce
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
copies of
\mathit{a}
respectively.
Atkey 2018
showed that the splitting lemma does not hold in a type system that is similar to ours, when parametrized over certain semirings. However, the splitting lemma holds for the preordered semirings we use for parametrizing LDC, i.e.,
\mathbb{N}_{=}
and
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
.

Lemma 3.3 (Splitting).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{A}
, then there exists
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Next, we look at weakening and substitution. For weakening, we add an extra assumption, whose allowed usage is
0
. For substitution, we need to ensure that the allowed usage of the variable matches the number of available copies of the substitute. Note that after substitution, the term needs the combined resources.

Lemma 3.4 (Weakening).

If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
, then
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:^{0}\mathit{C},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Lemma 3.5 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{C},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{C}
and
\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor
, then
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma,\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}\{\mathit{c}/\mathit{z}\}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Now, we consider the operational semantics of the language. Our operational semantics is a call-by-name small-step semantics. All the step rules are standard other than the
\beta
-rules that appear in Figure
5
. These rules ensure that the grade in the introduction form matches with that in the elimination form.

\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}

(Excerpt)

\frac{}{\vdash(\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b})\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}\leadsto\mathit{b}\{\mathit{a}/\mathit{x}\}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Step-AppBeta}{{{Step-AppBeta}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{}{\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}\leadsto\mathit{b}\{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}\}\{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}/\mathit{y}\}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Step-LetPairBeta}{{{Step-LetPairBeta}}}
}{},}

Figure 5
.
Small-step reduction for LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
)

With this operational semantics, our language enjoys the standard type soundness property.

Theorem 3.6 (Preservation).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a^{\prime}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Theorem 3.7 (Progress).

If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
, then either
\mathit{a}
is a value or there exists
\mathit{a^{\prime}}
such that
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
.

Now, consider the fact that in standard small-step semantics, we don’t substitute the free variables of the redex. So such a semantics cannot really model resource usage of programs. Environment-based semantics, where free variables of terms get substituted with values from the environment, are more amenable to modeling resource usage by programs. In Section
4
, we present an environment-based semantics for this calculus and show that the type system accounts usage correctly. But for now, we move on to dependency analysis.

3.3.
Type System for Dependency Analysis

Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\sqcap,\sqcup,\top,\bot,\sqsubseteq)
be an arbitrary lattice. (Technically, a lattice does not necessarily have top and bottom elements, but we can always add them.) We use
\ell,\mathit{m}
to denote elements of
L
. Now, interpreting
+,\cdot,0,1
and
<:
as
\sqcap,\sqcup,\top,\bot
and
\sqsubseteq
respectively, and using
q
and
r
for the elements of
\mathcal{L}
, we have a dependency calculus in the type system presented in Figure
4
. In Figure
6
, we present a few selected rules from this type system with just changed notation.

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}

(Selected rules)

\frac{}{\top\sqcup\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\ell}\mathit{A},\top\sqcup\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-VarD}{{{ST-VarD}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\ell\>\sqcup\>\mathit{m}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\ell}\mathit{B}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{m}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\ell}{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-LamD}{{{ST-LamD}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\ell}{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell\>\sqcup\>\mathit{m}}\mathit{A}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcap\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{m}}:^{\ell}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-AppD}{{{ST-AppD}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\ell\>\sqcup\>\mathit{m}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\ell}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcap\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{m}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{\ell}{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-PairD}{{{ST-PairD}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\ell\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}\>\sqcup\>\mathit{m}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\ell\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\ell}\mathit{B}\quad\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}\sqsubseteq\bot\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcap\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{m}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\ell}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-LetPairD}{{{ST-LetPairD}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}\quad\Gamma\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-SubLD}{{{ST-SubLD}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}\quad\ell\sqsubseteq\ell^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell^{\prime}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-SubRD}{{{ST-SubRD}}}
}{},}

Figure 6
.
Typing rules of LDC(
\mathcal{L}
)

The type system is now parametrized by
\mathcal{L}
in lieu of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
. We define
\ell\sqcup\Gamma
,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcap\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
\Gamma\sqsubseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
in the same way as their counterparts
\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma
,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
\Gamma<:\Gamma^{\prime}
respectively. The typing judgment
\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ldots,x_{n}:^{\ell_{n}}A_{n}\vdash b:^{\ell}B
may be read as:
\mathit{b}
is observable at
\ell
, assuming
x_{i}
is observable at
\ell_{i}
, for
i=1,2,\ldots,n
. With this reading, let us consider some of the typing rules.

The rule
ST-VarD
is as expected. The rule
ST-LamD
is interesting. The type
{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
contains functions that take arguments which are
\mathit{m}
-secure, i.e. observable only at
\mathit{m}
and higher levels. The body of such a function, therefore, needs to be checked setting the observability level of the argument at least as high as
\mathit{m}
.

Conversely in rule
ST-AppD
, the argument
\mathit{a}
needs to be observable at
\mathit{m}
or higher levels only. Note that the context in the conclusion judgment is formed by taking pointwise meet of the contexts checking the function and the argument. This ensures that no subterm that is observable in the premises becomes unobservable in the conclusion.

The rule
ST-WPairD
shows how to embox a secure term in a potentially insecure world. If
\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
is
\mathit{m}
-secure, then we can release it at level
\ell
, but only after putting it in a box that may be opened at
\mathit{m}
and higher levels. This is similar to how the modal type
T_{\mathit{m}}\>\mathit{A}
of Sealing Calculus, essentially
{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathbf{Unit}
, protects information, as we saw in rule
SC-Seal
.

Conversely, the rule
ST-LetPairD
ensures that an
\mathit{m}
-secure box may be opened only at
\mathit{m}
and higher levels. Note that in this rule, the side-condition
\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}\sqsubseteq\bot
forces
\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}
to be
\bot
because
\bot
is the bottom element. So rule
ST-LetPairD
may be simplified with
\ell_{{\mathrm{0}}}
set to
\bot
. However, we present the rule in this way to emphasize the similarity between LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
) and LDC(
\mathcal{L}
).

Next, we consider a few examples of derivable and non-derivable terms in LDC(
\mathcal{L}_{\diamond}
), where
\mathcal{L}_{\diamond}
is the diamond lattice (see Section
2.1
).

Let
\mathbf{Bool}:=\mathbf{Unit}+\mathbf{Unit}
and let
\mathbf{true}:=\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathbf{unit}
and
\mathbf{false}:=\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathbf{unit}
. Then,

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{\mathbf{L}}\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}:^{\mathbf{H}}{}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool}\text{ but }\emptyset\nvdash\lambda^{\mathbf{H}}\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}:^{\mathbf{L}}{}^{\mathbf{H}}\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool}

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{\mathbf{H}}\mathit{x}.\eta_{\mathbf{M_{1}}}\>\eta_{\mathbf{M_{2}}}\>\mathit{x}:^{\mathbf{L}}{}^{\mathbf{H}}\mathbf{Bool}\to T_{\mathbf{M_{1}}}\>T_{\mathbf{M_{2}}}\>\mathbf{Bool}\text{ but }\mathit{x}:^{\mathbf{H}}\mathbf{Bool}\nvdash\mathit{x}:^{\mathbf{M_{1}}}\mathbf{Bool}

Here,
T_{\mathit{m}}\>\mathit{A}:={}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathbf{Unit}
and
\eta_{\ell}\>\mathit{a}:=(\mathit{a}^{\ell},\mathbf{unit})
. On a closer look, we find that the non-derivable terms violate the dependency constraints modeled by
\mathcal{L}_{\diamond}
. The first term transfers information from
\mathbf{H}
to
\mathbf{L}
while the second one does so from
\mathbf{H}
to
\mathbf{M_{1}}
. The derivable terms, on the other hand, respect the dependency constraints modeled by
\mathcal{L}_{\diamond}
. The first term transfers information from
\mathbf{L}
to
\mathbf{H}
while the second one emboxes
\mathbf{H}
information in an
\mathbf{M_{2}}
box nested within an
\mathbf{M_{1}}
box.

Below, we present the terms that witness the standard join and fork operations in LDC(
\mathcal{L}
). (Note that any erased annotation is assumed to be
\bot
.) Recall that existing graded-context type systems cannot derive such a join operator. LDC(
\mathcal{L}
), though similar to these type systems, can do so owing to its flexible typing judgment, which allows one to vary the observer’s level.

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash\mathit{c_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\>T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\text{ and }\emptyset\vdash\mathit{c_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\>T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\to T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>T_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{A}\text{ where,}

\displaystyle\mathit{c_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:=\lambda\mathit{x}.\eta_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>(\mathbf{let}\>(\mathit{y}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\_)\>\mathbf{be}\>(\mathbf{let}\>(\mathit{z}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\_)\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{z})\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{y})

\displaystyle\mathit{c_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:=\lambda\mathit{x}.\eta_{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\eta_{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>(\mathbf{let}\>(\mathit{y}^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\_)\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{y})

The derivations of these terms appear in Appendix
J
.

Next, we look at the metatheory of LDC(
\mathcal{L}
).

3.4.
Metatheory of Dependency Analysis

We consider the dependency counterparts of the properties of LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
), presented in Section
3.2
. Most of these properties are true of LDC(
\mathcal{L}
). LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) satisfies the multiplication lemma. The lemma says that we can always simultaneously upgrade the context and the level at which the derived term is observed.

Lemma 3.8 (Multiplication).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
, then
\mathit{m_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\sqcup\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{m_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell}\mathit{A}
.

The splitting lemma is also true. Since
\sqcap
is idempotent, it follows directly from rule
ST-SubRD
.

Lemma 3.9 (Splitting).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{A}
, then there exists
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcap\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

The factorization lemma, however, is not true here because if true, it would allow secure information to leak. To see how, consider the following LDC(
\mathcal{L}_{\diamond}
) judgment:
\emptyset\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{let}\>(\mathit{y}^{\mathbf{H}},\_)\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{y}:^{\mathbf{H}}T_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool}
. If the factorization lemma were true, from this judgment, we would have:
\emptyset\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\mathbf{let}\>(\mathit{y}^{\mathbf{H}},\_)\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{y}:^{\mathbf{L}}T_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathbf{Bool}\to\mathbf{Bool}
, a non-constant function from
T_{\mathbf{H}}\>\mathbf{Bool}
to
\mathbf{Bool}
in an
\mathbf{L}
-secure world, representing a leak of secure information. Note that in LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
), the factorization lemma is required for proving the splitting lemma which, in turn, is necessary to show substitution. In LDC(
\mathcal{L}
), the splitting lemma holds trivially and does not need any factorization lemma.

Next, we consider weakening and substitution. The weakening lemma adds an extra assumption at the highest security level to the context. The substitution lemma, on the other hand, substitutes an assumption held at
\mathit{m_{{\mathrm{0}}}}
with a term derived at
\mathit{m_{{\mathrm{0}}}}
.

Lemma 3.10 (Weakening).

If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
, then
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:^{\top}\mathit{C},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
.

Lemma 3.11 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:^{\mathit{m_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{C},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:^{\mathit{m_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{C}
and
\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor
, then
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcap\Gamma,\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}\{\mathit{c}/\mathit{z}\}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
.

Now, LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) can be given the same operational semantics as LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
). With respect to this operational semantics, LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) enjoys the standard type soundness property.

Theorem 3.12 (Preservation).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
and
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a^{\prime}}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
.

Theorem 3.13 (Progress).

If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
, then either
\mathit{a}
is a value or there exists
\mathit{a^{\prime}}
such that
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
.

Type soundness is not strong enough to show that LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) analyses dependencies correctly. For that, we need to show the calculus passes the noninterference test, which ensures that variations in
\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
-inputs do not affect
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
-outputs, whenever
\neg(\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
.
In Section
4
, we shall prove that LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) passes the noninterference test. But before that, we discuss the relation between LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) and Sealing Calculus.

3.5.
Sealing Calculus and LDC

The Sealing Calculus
(
Shikuma and
Igarashi 2006
)
embeds into LDC(
\mathcal{L}
). We don’t provide details of the embedding here because LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) is the same as SDC of
Choudhury
et al. 2022
.
Choudhury
et al. 2022
show that the Sealing Calculus embeds into SDC. Note however that SDC can only be parametrized over lattices and not over semirings that help track linearity.

A technical difference between LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) and SDC is that SDC does not have security annotations on function and product types. However, SDC has modal types,
T_{\ell}\>\mathit{A}
. Annotated function and product types of LDC(
\mathcal{L}
),
{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{B}
, correspond to types
T_{\mathit{m}}\>\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
T_{\mathit{m}}\>\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{B}
respectively in SDC. Conversely, the modal type of SDC,
T_{\mathit{m}}\>\mathit{A}
, corresponds to
{}^{\mathit{m}}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathbf{Unit}
in LDC(
\mathcal{L}
).

Given that Sealing Calculus is a general dependency calculus that embeds into LDC(
\mathcal{L}
), we conclude that LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) is also a general dependency calculus. Additionally, LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
) can analyze resource usage. Thus, LDC can be used for both usage and dependency analyses by parametrizing the calculus over appropriate structures. In the next section, we prove correctness of these analyses in LDC through a heap semantics for the calculus.

4.
Heap Semantics for LDC

LDC models resource usage and information flow. Standard operational semantics cannot enforce constraints on usage and flow. However, an environment-based semantics, for example a heap semantics, can do so. As such, in this section, we present a weighted-heap-based semantics for LDC. We shall use the same heap semantics for analyzing both resource usage and information flow in LDC, just as we used the same standard small-step semantics for both the analyses.

Heap-based semantics have been used for analyzing resource usage in literature
(
Turner and Wadler 1999
;
Choudhury et al. 2021
;
Marshall
et al. 2022
)
. Our heap semantics follows
Choudhury et al. 2021
. The difference between our heap semantics and that of
Choudhury et al. 2021
is that we use it for both usage and flow analyses while they use it for usage analysis only. Other than this difference, both the semantics are essentially the same.

Heap semantics shows how a term reduces in a heap that assigns values to the free variables of the term. Heaps are ordered lists of variable-term pairs, where the terms may be seen as the definitions of the corresponding variables. To every variable-term pair in a heap, we assign a weight, which may be either a
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
or an
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
. A heap where every variable-term pair has a weight associated with it is referred to as a weighted heap. We assume that our weighted heaps satisfy the following two properties: uniqueness, meaning, a variable is not defined twice; and acyclicity, meaning, definition of a variable does not refer to itself or to other variables appearing subsequently in the heap. Next, we model reductions in terms of interactions between terms and weighted heaps that define the free variables of terms.

4.1.
Reduction Relation

The heap-based reduction rules appear in Figure
7
. There are a few things to note with regard to this reduction relation,
[\mathit{H}]\mathit{a}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{a^{\prime}}
:

•

\mathit{S}
here denotes a support set
(
Pitts 2013
)
of variables that must be avoided while choosing fresh names.

•

From a resource usage perspective, the judgment above may be read as:
\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{a}
use resources from heap
\mathit{H}
to produce
\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{a^{\prime}}
, with
\mathit{H^{\prime}}
being the left-over resources. Regarding a heap as a memory, usage of resources corresponds to memory look-up during reduction.

•

From an information flow perspective, the judgment may be read as: under the security constraints of
\mathit{H}
, the term
\mathit{a}
steps to
\mathit{a^{\prime}}
at security level
\mathit{q}
with
\mathit{H^{\prime}}
being the updated security constraints. Regarding a heap as a memory, security labels on assignments correspond to access permissions on data while the label on the judgment corresponds to the security clearance of the user.

Heap,
H::=\emptyset\>|\>\mathit{H},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{q}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a}

[\mathit{H}]\mathit{a}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{a^{\prime}}

(Selected Heap Step Rules)

\frac{\mathit{q}\neq 0}{[\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r}+\mathit{q}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a},\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{x}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a},\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{a}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapStep-Var}{{{HeapStep-Var}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{[\mathit{H}]\mathit{a}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{a^{\prime}}\quad\mathit{q}<:\mathit{q^{\prime}}}{[\mathit{H}]\mathit{a}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q^{\prime}}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{a^{\prime}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapStep-Discard}{{{HeapStep-Discard}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{[\mathit{H}]\mathit{b}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}\,\cup\,\textit{fv}\>\mathit{a}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{b^{\prime}}}{[\mathit{H}]\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{b^{\prime}}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapStep-AppL}{{{HeapStep-AppL}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\mathit{y}\>\textit{fresh}\quad\mathit{b^{\prime}}=\mathit{b}\{\mathit{y}/\mathit{x}\}}{[\mathit{H}](\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b})\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H},\mathit{y}\overset{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a}]\mathit{b^{\prime}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapStep-AppBeta}{{{HeapStep-AppBeta}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\mathit{x^{\prime}}\>\textit{fresh}\quad\mathit{y^{\prime}}\>\textit{fresh}\quad\mathit{b^{\prime}}=\mathit{b}\{\mathit{x^{\prime}}/\mathit{x}\}\{\mathit{y^{\prime}}/\mathit{y}\}}{[\mathit{H}]\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H},\mathit{x^{\prime}}\overset{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y^{\prime}}\overset{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{b^{\prime}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapStep-LetPairBeta}{{{HeapStep-LetPairBeta}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\mathit{x^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\textit{fresh}\quad\mathit{b^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\{\mathit{x^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\}}{[\mathit{H}]\mathbf{case}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H},\mathit{x^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\overset{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}]\mathit{b^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapStep-Case1Beta}{{{HeapStep-Case1Beta}}}
}{},}

Figure 7
.
Heap Semantics for LDC (Excerpt)

Now, we consider some of the reduction rules, presented in Figure
7
. The most interesting of the rules is rule
HeapStep-Var
. From a resource usage perspective, this rule may be read as: to step
\mathit{q}
copies of
\mathit{x}
, we need to look-up the value of
\mathit{x}
for
