\mathit{q}
times, thereby using up
\mathit{q}
resources. From an information flow perspective, this rule may be read as: the data pointed to by
\mathit{x}
, held at security level
\mathit{r}\>\sqcap\>\mathit{q}
, is observable to
\mathit{q}
since
\mathit{r}\>\sqcap\>\mathit{q}\sqsubseteq\mathit{q}
. Note that since
\mathit{r}\>\sqcap\>\mathit{q}\sqsubseteq\mathit{r}
, the security level of the assignment cannot go down in the updated heap. However, it can always remain the same because
(\mathit{r}\>\sqcap\>\mathit{q})\>\sqcap\>\mathit{q}=\mathit{r}\>\sqcap\>\mathit{q}
. The rule
HeapStep-Var
includes the precondition
\mathit{q}\neq 0
because at world
0
or
\top
, usage and flow constraints are not meaningful.

The rule
HeapStep-Discard
is also interesting. From a resource usage perspective, it enables discarding of resources, whenever permitted. From an information flow perspective, it corresponds to information remaining visible to an observer as the observer’s security clearance goes up.

The rule
HeapStep-AppL
shows how
\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}
steps, as
\mathit{b}
steps. Note that the support set in the premise contains the free variables of the argument to ensure that they are avoided while choosing fresh names. We omit the left rules corresponding to other elimination forms, which are as expected.

In the beta rules
HeapStep-AppBeta
,
HeapStep-LetPairBeta
, and
HeapStep-Case1Beta
, we add new assignments to the heap avoiding variable capture. The weight at which a new assignment is added is decided by the annotation on the term and the label on the judgment. For example, in rule
HeapStep-AppBeta
, the new assumption is added at
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}
or
\mathit{q}\>\sqcup\>\mathit{r}
, since the annotation on the application is
\mathit{r}
and the label on the judgment is
\mathit{q}
.

With these rules, let us look at some reductions that go through and some that don’t.

\displaystyle[\mathit{x}\overset{1}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathit{x}\Longrightarrow^{1}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{x}\overset{0}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathbf{true}

\displaystyle\text{ but not }[\mathit{x}\overset{0}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathit{x}\Longrightarrow^{1}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{x}\overset{0}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathbf{true}

\displaystyle[\mathit{x}\overset{2}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathit{x}\Longrightarrow^{2}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{x}\overset{0}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathbf{true}

\displaystyle\text{ but not }[\mathit{x}\overset{1}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathit{x}\Longrightarrow^{2}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{x}\overset{0}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathbf{true}

\displaystyle[\mathit{x}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathit{x}\Longrightarrow^{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{x}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathbf{true}

\displaystyle\text{ but not }[\mathit{x}\overset{\mathbf{H}}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathit{x}\Longrightarrow^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{x}\overset{\mathbf{H}}{\mapsto}\mathbf{true}]\mathbf{true}

4.2.
Ensuring Fair Usage and Secure Flow

Looking at the rules in Figure
7
, we observe that they enforce fairness of resource usage. The only rule that allows usage of resources is rule
HeapStep-Var
. This rule ensures that a look-up goes through only when the environment can provide adequate resources. It also takes away the necessary resources from the environment after a successful look-up. The rules in Figure
7
also ensure security of information flow. The only rule that allows information to flow from heap to program is again rule
HeapStep-Var
. This rule ensures that information can flow through only when the user has the necessary permission.

The following two lemma formalize the arguments presented above. The first lemma says that a definition that is not available at some point during reduction does not become available at a later point. The second lemma says that an unavailable definition does not play any role in reduction. Note that in case of information flow, the constraint
\neg(\exists q_{0},r=q+q_{0})
is equivalent to
\neg(\mathit{r}\sqsubseteq\mathit{q})
. (Here,
\lvert\mathit{H}\rvert
denotes the length of
\mathit{H}
.)

Lemma 4.1 (Unchanged).

If
[\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a},\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{c}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r^{\prime}}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a},\mathit{H^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{c^{\prime}}
(where
\lvert H_{1}\rvert=\lvert H^{\prime}_{1}\rvert
) and
\neg(\exists q_{0},r=q+q_{0})
, then
\mathit{r^{\prime}}=r
.

Lemma 4.2 (Irrelevant).

If
[\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a},\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{c}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}\,\cup\,\textit{fv}\>\mathit{b}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r^{\prime}}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a},\mathit{H^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{c^{\prime}}
(where
\lvert\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rvert=\lvert\mathit{H^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rvert
) and
\neg(\exists q_{0},r=q+q_{0})
, then
[\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r}}{\mapsto}\mathit{b},\mathit{H_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{c}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}\,\cup\,\textit{fv}\>\mathit{a}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{r^{\prime}}}{\mapsto}\mathit{b},\mathit{H^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathit{c^{\prime}}
.

These lemmas, in conjunction with the soundness theorem we present next, shall guarantee fairness of resource usage and security of information flow in LDC.

4.3.
Soundness With Respect To Heap Semantics

The key idea behind usage analysis through heap semantics is that, if a heap contains the right amount of resources to evaluate some number of copies of a term, as judged by the type system, then the evaluation of that many number of copies of the term in that heap does not get stuck. Since the heap-based reduction rules enforce fairness of resource usage, this would mean that the type system accounts resource usage correctly.

The key idea behind dependency analysis through heap semantics is similar. If a heap sets the right access permissions for a user, as judged by the type system, then the evaluation, in that heap, of any program observable to that user does not get stuck. Since the reduction rules enforce security of information flow, this would mean that the type system allows only secure flows.

The compatibility relation,
\mathit{H}\models\Gamma
, between a heap
\mathit{H}
and a context
\Gamma
, formalizes the idea that the heap
\mathit{H}
contains the right amount of resources or has set the right access permissions for evaluating any term type-checked in context
\Gamma
. The compatibility relation
(
Choudhury et al. 2021
)
is defined below:

\mathit{H}\models\Gamma

(Compatibility)

\frac{}{\emptyset\models\emptyset}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapCompat-Empty}{{{HeapCompat-Empty}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\mathit{H}\models\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}{\mathit{H},\mathit{x}\overset{\mathit{q}}{\mapsto}\mathit{a}\models\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:HeapCompat-Cons}{{{HeapCompat-Cons}}}
}{},}

The soundness theorem stated next says that if a heap
\mathit{H}
is compatible with a context
\Gamma
, then the evaluation, starting with heap
\mathit{H}
, of a term type-checked in context
\Gamma
does not get stuck.

Theorem 4.3 (Soundness).

If
\mathit{H}\models\Gamma
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
q\neq 0
, then either
\mathit{a}
is a value or there exists
\mathit{H^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{a^{\prime}}
such that:

•

[\mathit{H}]\mathit{a}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{a^{\prime}}

•

\mathit{H^{\prime}}\models\Gamma^{\prime}

•

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a^{\prime}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}

Note here that the typing context gets updated with every step of reduction, unlike standard type preservation theorem. This is so because the term, as it reduces, needs less resources from the context. However, the updated context is always compatible with the updated heap. Further, note that the labels on the typing judgments and the stepping judgment are the same. From a resource usage perspective, this match-up corresponds to an invariance of the number of copies of the term during reduction. From an information flow perspective, this match-up corresponds to an invariance of the level of the observer during reduction.

Below, we present some corollaries of this soundness theorem.

Corollary 4.4 (No Usage).

In LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
): Let
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:^{1}{}^{0}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
. Then, for any
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{0}\mathit{A}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{0}\mathit{A}
, the terms
\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{0}
and
\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{0}
have the same operational behavior, i.e., either both the terms diverge or both reduce to the same value.

The above corollary also holds in LDC(
\mathcal{L}
) with
0
and
1
replaced by
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{L}
respectively. In LDC(
\mathcal{L}
), this corollary shows
non-interference
of high-security inputs in low-security outputs.

Corollary 4.5 (Noninterference).

In LDC(
\mathcal{L}
): Let
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:^{\mathbf{L}}{}^{\mathbf{H}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
. Then, for any
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathbf{H}}\mathit{A}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathbf{H}}\mathit{A}
, the terms
\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathbf{H}}
and
\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathbf{H}}
have the same operational behavior.

The soundness theorem may also be employed to reason about other usages, like affine usage in LDC(
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
).

Corollary 4.6 (Affine Usage).

In LDC(
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
): Let
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}
. Then, for any
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}\mathit{A}
, the term
\mathit{f}\>\mathit{a}^{1}
uses
\mathit{a}
at most once during reduction.

Now that we have seen the syntax and semantics of simply-typed version of LDC, we move on to its Pure Type System (PTS) version.

5.
Linearity and Dependency Analyses in Pure Type Systems

A Pure Type System (PTS) is characterized by a tuple,
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R})
, where
\mathcal{S}
is a set of sorts,
\mathcal{A}
is a set of axioms and
\mathcal{R}
is a ternary relation between sorts
(
Barendregt 1993
)
. Many type systems like simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus, System F, System
\text{F}{\omega}
, Calculus of Constructions, Type-in-Type, etc. may be seen as PTSs. Note that a PTS need not be normalizing, for example, Type-in-Type allows nonterminating computations. We parametrize LDC over an abstract PTS so that it may be instantiated to particular PTSs as required.

5.1.
Simple Version Vs PTS Version

The PTS version of LDC is similar to its simply-typed version. As far as types and terms are concerned, we just need to add to them the sorts in
\mathcal{S}
and generalize
{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{B}
to
\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}
and
\Sigma\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}
respectively. For resource usage and information flow analyses, we use the same parametrizing structures, i.e.
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathcal{L}
respectively. However, there is an important distinction between these two versions. In the PTS version, we need to extend our analyses from terms to both types and terms.

The key idea behind our extension is that usage and flow analyses for types and terms can be carried out separately. This idea is inspired by recent developments in graded-context dependent type systems
(
McBride 2016
;
Atkey 2018
;
Choudhury et al. 2021
;
Moon
et al. 2021
)
.
McBride 2016
first noted that linearity and dependent types can be smoothly combined by distinguishing between ‘contemplative use’ of resources in types and ‘consumptive use’ of resources in terms.
Atkey 2018
carried this work forward with the calculus
QTT
, where types live in a resource-agnostic world and terms live in a resource-aware world.
Choudhury et al. 2021
presented an alternative system,
GraD
, where both types and terms live in a resource-aware world but resources used by a type are zeroed-out while calculating resources used by terms of that type.
Moon
et al. 2021
presented yet another alternative system,
Grtt
, where resources used by types are not zeroed-out but tracked simultaneously along with the resources used by terms. In its analysis,
GraD
is more uniform than
QTT
and much simpler than
Grtt
. So LDC analyzes usage in types à la
GraD
.

We now look at the type system of the calculus.

5.2.
Type System of LDC

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}

(PTS version)

\frac{\mathit{c}:\mathit{s}\in\mathcal{A}}{\emptyset\vdash\mathit{c}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Axiom}{{{PTS-Axiom}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Delta\vdash_{0}\mathit{A}:\mathit{s}\quad\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta}{0\cdot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Var}{{{PTS-Var}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\quad\Delta\vdash_{0}\mathit{B}:\mathit{s}\quad\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta}{\Gamma,\mathit{y}:^{0}\mathit{B}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Weak}{{{PTS-Weak}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{A}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{B}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{3}}}})\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Pi}{{{PTS-Pi}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\quad\Delta\vdash_{0}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}:\mathit{s}\quad\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Lam}{{{PTS-Lam}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\{\mathit{a}/\mathit{x}\}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-App}{{{PTS-App}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\quad\Delta\vdash_{0}\mathit{B}:\mathit{s}\quad\mathit{A}=_{\beta}\mathit{B}\quad\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Conv}{{{PTS-Conv}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{3}}}})\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\Sigma\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Sigma}{{{PTS-Sigma}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Delta\vdash_{0}\Sigma\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{s}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\{\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{x}\}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor=\Delta}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{\mathit{q}}\Sigma\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Pair}{{{PTS-Pair}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Delta,\mathit{z}:\Sigma\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash_{0}\mathit{B}:\mathit{s}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\Sigma\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\{(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})/\mathit{z}\}\quad\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor=\Delta}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\{\mathit{a}/\mathit{z}\}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-LetPair}{{{PTS-LetPair}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{s}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Sum}{{{PTS-Sum}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Delta\vdash_{0}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{s}\quad\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\quad\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta}{\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Inj1}{{{PTS-Inj1}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Delta,\mathit{z}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash_{0}\mathit{B}:\mathit{s}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\{\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}/\mathit{z}\}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\{\mathbf{inj}_{2}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}/\mathit{z}\}\quad\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor=\Delta}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{case}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\{\mathit{a}/\mathit{z}\}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-Case}{{{PTS-Case}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\quad\Gamma<:\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-SubL}{{{PTS-SubL}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\quad\mathit{q}<:\mathit{q^{\prime}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q^{\prime}}}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-SubR}{{{PTS-SubR}}}
}{},}

Figure 8
.
Type System for LDC (Excerpt)

\mathit{A}=_{\beta}\mathit{B}

(Definitional Equality)

\frac{\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{B_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Eq-PiCong}{{{Eq-PiCong}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Eq-LamCong}{{{Eq-LamCong}}}
}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\quad\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=_{\beta}\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}{\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}}=_{\beta}\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}^{\mathit{r}}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:Eq-AppCong}{{{Eq-AppCong}}}
}{},}

Figure 9
.
Equality Rules for LDC (Excerpt)

The typing and equality rules appear in Figures
8
and
9
respectively. There are a few things to note:

•

We use these rules for both resource usage analysis and information flow analysis (with appropriate interpretations of
+,\cdot,0,1
and
<:
).

•

The judgment
\Delta\vdash_{0}\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}
is shorthand for the judgment
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{0}\mathit{A}
where
\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta
and
\overline{\Gamma}
is a
\mathbf{0}
vector. Note that this judgment is essentially the standard typing judgment
\Delta\vdash\mathit{a}:\>\mathit{A}
because in world
0
, neither resource usage nor information flow constraints are meaningful.

•

We track usage and flow in terms and types separately. The rule
PTS-Var
illustrates this principle nicely. The type
\mathit{A}
may use some resources or be observable at some low security level. But while type-checking a term of type
\mathit{A}
, we zero-out the requirements of
\mathit{A}
or set
\mathit{A}
to the highest security level. This principle also applies to several other rules, for example, rule
PTS-Lam
, rule
PTS-Pair
, etc.

•

The rule
PTS-Pi
shows how we track usage and flow in types. This rule brings out an important aspect of our analysis: not only do we separate the analysis in types and terms but also we allow a term and its type to treat the same bound variable differently. The annotation on the type,
\mathit{r}
in this case, shows how the bound variable is used in the body of a term having that type. This annotation is
not
related to how the bound variable is used in the body of the type itself.

Let us consider an example: the polymorphic identity type,
\Pi\mathit{x}:^{0}\!\mathit{s}.\Pi\mathit{y}:^{1}\!\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}
, uses the bound variable
\mathit{x}
in its body but a function having this type (e.g. polymorphic identity function
\lambda^{0}\mathit{x}.\lambda^{1}\mathit{y}.\mathit{y}
) can not use the bound variable
\mathit{x}
in its body.

•

We use
\beta
-equivalence for equality in rule
PTS-Conv
. It is a congruent, equivalence relation closed under
\beta
-reduction of terms. Some of the equality rules appear in Figure
9
. They are mostly standard. However, the congruence rules
Eq-PiCong
,
Eq-LamCong
, and
Eq-AppCong
need to check that the grade annotations on the terms being equated match up.

Next, we look at the metatheory of the calculus.

5.3.
Metatheory of LDC

The PTS version of LDC satisfies the PTS analogues of all the lemmas and theorems satisfied by the simply-typed version, presented in Sections
3.2
and
3.4
. The PTS version also enjoys the same standard call-by-name semantics as the simply-typed version. Further, the PTS version is type-sound with respect to this semantics.

Next, we state the PTS analogues of some of the crucial lemmas and theorems presented in Sections
3.2
and
3.4
.

Lemma 5.1 (Substitution).

If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{z}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{C},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{C}
and
\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor
, then
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma,\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\{\mathit{c}/\mathit{z}\}\vdash\mathit{a}\{\mathit{c}/\mathit{z}\}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\{\mathit{c}/\mathit{z}\}
.

Theorem 5.2 (Preservation).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a^{\prime}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Theorem 5.3 (Progress).

If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
, then either
\mathit{a}
is a value or there exists
\mathit{a^{\prime}}
such that
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
.

Next, we consider heap semantics for LDC.

5.4.
Heap Semantics for LDC

The PTS version of LDC enjoys the same heap reduction relation as its simply-typed counterpart. However, the PTS version presents a challenge with regard to well-typedness of terms during reduction. In the simply-typed version, we could delay substitutions in a term by loading them into the heap without being concerned about how it might affect the type of that term. In the PTS version, delayed substitutions may cause the term to ‘lag behind’ the type. We consider an example from
Choudhury et al. 2021
that illustrates this point.

The polymorphic identity function,
\lambda^{0}\mathit{x}:\mathit{s}.\lambda^{1}\mathit{y}:\mathit{x}.\mathit{y}
, has type
\Pi\mathit{x}:^{0}\!\mathit{s}.\Pi\mathit{y}:^{1}\!\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}
. Instantiating the function at
\mathbf{Unit}
, we get
(\lambda^{0}\mathit{x}:\mathit{s}.\lambda^{1}\mathit{y}:\mathit{x}.\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{Unit}^{0}
of type
\Pi\mathit{y}:^{1}\!\mathbf{Unit}.\mathbf{Unit}
. Now,
[\emptyset](\lambda^{0}\mathit{x}:\mathit{s}.\lambda^{1}\mathit{y}:\mathit{x}.\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{Unit}^{0}\Longrightarrow^{1}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{x}\overset{0}{\mapsto}\mathbf{Unit}]\lambda^{1}\mathit{y}:\mathit{x}.\mathit{y}
. Unless we look at the definition in the heap, we have no reason to believe that
\lambda^{1}\mathit{y}:\mathit{x}.\mathit{y}
has type
\Pi\mathit{y}:^{1}\!\mathbf{Unit}.\mathbf{Unit}
. The delayed substitution
\mathit{x}\overset{0}{\mapsto}\mathbf{Unit}
causes the term
\lambda^{1}\mathit{y}:\mathit{x}.\mathit{y}
to lag behind the type
\Pi\mathit{y}:^{1}\!\mathbf{Unit}.\mathbf{Unit}
. Note that this challenge arises due to delayed substitution only and is independent of usage and flow analyses.

To overcome this challenge,
Choudhury et al. 2021
use the following strategy. First, they extend their type system with contexts that allow definitions. Then, they show that the extended calculus is sound with respect to heap semantics. Thereafter, they prove the original calculus equivalent to the extended calculus. Using this equivalence, they conclude that the original calculus is also sound with respect to heap semantics. We use the same strategy for LDC. Owing to space constraints, we omit the details in the main body of the paper. For details, the interested reader may please refer to Appendix
F
.

LDC is sound with respect to heap semantics. Note the statement below is the same as its simply-typed counterpart.

Theorem 5.4 (Soundness).

If
\mathit{H}\models\Gamma
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
q\neq 0
, then either
\mathit{a}
is a value or there exists
\mathit{H^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{a^{\prime}}
such that
[\mathit{H}]\mathit{a}\Longrightarrow^{\mathit{q}}_{\mathit{S}}[\mathit{H^{\prime}}]\mathit{a^{\prime}}
and
\mathit{H^{\prime}}\models\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a^{\prime}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

The corollaries of the soundness theorem presented in Section
4.3
also hold for the PTS version. Below, we present some corollaries that are related to polymorphic types.

Corollary 5.5.

In LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
): If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:^{1}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{0}\!\mathit{s}.\mathit{x}
and
\emptyset\vdash_{0}\mathit{A}:\mathit{s}
, then
\mathit{f}\>\mathit{A}^{0}
must diverge.

Corollary 5.6.

In LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
): In a strongly normalizing PTS, if
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:^{1}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{0}\!\mathit{s}.\Pi\mathit{y}:^{1}\!\mathit{x}.\mathit{x}
and
\emptyset\vdash_{0}\mathit{A}:\mathit{s}
and
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}\mathit{A}
, then
\mathit{f}\>\mathit{A}^{0}\>\mathit{a}^{1}=_{\beta}\mathit{a}
.

6.
Adding Unrestricted Usage

Till now, we used LDC for analyzing exact usage, bounded usage and dependency. In this section, we use the calculus for analyzing unrestricted usage as well. Unrestricted usage, referred to by
\omega
, is different from exact and bounded usage, referred to by
n\in\mathbb{N}
, in two ways:

•

\omega
is an additive annihilator, meaning
\omega+q=q+\omega=\omega
, for all
q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
.

No
n\in\mathbb{N}
is an additive annihilator.

•

\omega
is a multiplicative annihilator (almost) as well, meaning
\omega\cdot\mathit{q}=\mathit{q}\cdot\omega=\omega
for
q\in(\mathbb{N}-\{0\})\cup\{\omega\}
. No
n\in\mathbb{N}-\{0\}
is a multiplicative annihilator.

To accommodate this behavior of
\omega
, we need to make a change to our type system. But before we make this change, let us fix our preordered semirings:

•

\mathbb{N}_{=}^{\omega}
, that contains
\omega
and the preordered semiring
\mathbb{N}_{=}
, with
\omega<:\mathit{q}
for all
q

•

\mathbb{N}_{\geq}^{\omega}
, that contains
\omega
and the preordered semiring
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
, with
\omega<:\mathit{q}
for all
q

•

\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
, described in Section
2.2

We use
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
to denote an arbitrary member of the above set of semirings. Next, we discuss the change necessary as we move from LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
) to LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
).

6.1.
The Problem and its Solution

When unrestricted usage is allowed, the type systems in Figures
4
and
8
cannot enforce fairness of resource usage. Consider the following ‘unfair’ derivation allowed by the simple type system:

\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.(\mathit{x}^{1},\mathit{x}):^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.(\mathit{x}^{1},\mathit{x}):^{\omega}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}\mathit{x}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}\vdash(\mathit{x}^{1},\mathit{x}):^{\omega}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathit{x}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}\mathit{x}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}

The judgment
\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.(\mathit{x}^{1},\mathit{x}):^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}
is unfair because it allows copying of resources. Carefully observing the derivation, we find that the unfairness arises when
\omega
‘tricks’ the
ST-Lam
rule into believing that the term uses
\mathit{x}
once.

This unfairness leads to a failure in type soundness. To see how, consider the term:
\mathit{y}:^{1}\mathit{A}\vdash(\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.(\mathit{x}^{1},\mathit{x}))\>\mathit{y}^{1}:^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}
that type-checks via the above derivation and rule
ST-App
. This term steps to:

\vdash(\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.(\mathit{x}^{1},\mathit{x}))\>\mathit{y}^{1}\leadsto(\mathit{y}^{1},\mathit{y})
. But then, we have unsoundness because:
\mathit{y}:^{1}\mathit{A}\nvdash(\mathit{y}^{1},\mathit{y}):^{1}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\>\times\>\mathit{A}
. Therefore, to ensure type soundness, we need to modify rule
ST-Lam
and rule
PTS-Lam
.

We modify these rules as follows:

\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathit{q}=\omega\Rightarrow\mathit{r}=\omega\hskip 8.19447pt\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\neq 0}{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-LamOmega}{{{ST-LamOmega}}}
}{},}

\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\hskip 8.19447pt\Delta\vdash_{0}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}:\mathit{s}\hskip 8.19447pt\lfloor\Gamma\rfloor=\Delta\hskip 8.19447pt\mathit{q}=\omega\Rightarrow\mathit{r}=\omega\hskip 8.19447pt\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\neq 0}{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\Pi\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}.\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:PTS-LamOmega}{{{PTS-LamOmega}}}
}{},}

There are several points to note regarding the above rules:

•

Rules
ST-LamOmega
and
PTS-LamOmega
are generalizations of rules
ST-Lam
and
PTS-Lam
respectively, meaning, when the grades are restricted to natural numbers, replacing rules
ST-Lam
and
PTS-Lam
with rules
ST-LamOmega
and
PTS-LamOmega
has no effect on the type system.

•

These rules impose the constraint:
\mathit{q}=\omega\Rightarrow\mathit{r}=\omega
. This way
\omega
won’t be able to ‘trick’ the Lambda-rule into believing that functions use their arguments less than what they actually do. In particular, with these modified rules, the above unfair derivation won’t go through.

•

The constraint
\mathit{q}=\omega\Rightarrow\mathit{r}=\omega
, while required for blocking unfair derivations, also blocks some fair ones like the one below:

\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{x}:^{\omega}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}\mathit{x}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}

To allow such derivations, while still imposing this constraint, rules
ST-LamOmega
and
PTS-LamOmega
multiply the conclusion judgment by
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}
. This multiplication helps these rules allow the above derivation as:

\emptyset\vdash\lambda^{1}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{x}:^{\omega}{}^{1}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{A}\mathit{x}:^{1}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{1}\mathit{A}

•

The side condition
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\neq 0
makes sure that a meaningful judgment is not turned into a meaningless one. Recall that judgments in
0
world are meaningless, as far as linearity and dependency analyses are concerned.

•

Rule
ST-LamOmega
can also be equivalently replaced with the following two simpler rules (and similarly for rule
PTS-LamOmega
):

\frac{\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathit{q}=\omega\Rightarrow\mathit{r}=\omega}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-LamOmega0}{{{ST-LamOmega0}}}
}{},}\hskip 10.0pt\frac{\omega\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}\hskip 8.19447pt\mathit{q}\neq 0}{\omega\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\omega}\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:ST-Omega}{{{ST-Omega}}}
}{},}

Replacing rules
ST-Lam
and
PTS-Lam
with rules
ST-LamOmega
and
PTS-LamOmega
is the only modification that we need to make to the type systems presented in Figures
4
and
8
in order to enable them track unrestricted usage.

With this modification to the type system, LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
) satisfies all the lemmas and theorems satisfied by LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}
). In particular, LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
) satisfies type soundness (Theorems
5.2
and
5.3
) and heap soundness (Theorem
5.4
). We state this property as a theorem below.

Theorem 6.1.

LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
) satisfies type soundness and heap soundness.

Thus, LDC is a general linear dependency calculus. For tracking linearity, we can use any of the
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
s. For tracking dependency, we can use any lattice. For tracking linearity and dependency simultaneously, we can use the cartesian product of these structures. Below, we take up some examples that illustrate combined linearity and dependency analysis in LDC.

6.2.
Combined Linearity and Dependency Analysis

First, note that even though preordered semirings and lattices have irreconcilably different sets of axioms, as described in Section
2.3
, they are still algebraic structures with two binary operators and a binary order relation. Therefore, one can define the cartesian product of these two structures. Concretely, given a preordered semiring
\mathcal{Q}=(Q,+,\cdot,0,1,<:)
and a lattice
\mathcal{L}=(L,\sqcap,\sqcup,\top,\bot,\sqsubseteq)
, one can define
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{L}
, the cartesian product of
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{L}
, as the set
Q\times L
, together with:

•

two constants,
0
and
1
, defined as:
0\triangleq(0,\top)
and
1\triangleq(1,\bot)
;

•

a binary operator,
+
, defined as:
(q_{1},\ell_{1})\,\mathbf{+}\,(q_{2},\ell_{2})\triangleq(\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcap\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
;

•

another binary operator,
\mathbf{\cdot}
, defined as:
(q_{1},\ell_{1})\,\mathbf{\cdot}\,(q_{2},\ell_{2})\triangleq(\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\>\sqcup\>\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}})
; and

•

a binary order relation,
\mathbf{<:}
, defined as:
(q_{1},\ell_{1})\,\mathbf{<:}\,(q_{2},\ell_{2})\triangleq\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}<:\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\text{ and }\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Now, given any
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
and
\mathcal{L}
, we can parametrize LDC over
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}\times\mathcal{L}
, using the above interpretation of
+,\cdot,0,1
and
<:
. Such parametrization helps us combine linearity and dependency analyses. To illustrate, let
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
(or
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Aff}}
) and
\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{L}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{M}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{H}
. Then, in LDC(
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{L}
) (extended with
\mathbf{Int}
type), we have:

\displaystyle\mathit{x}:^{(\omega,\mathbf{L})}\mathbf{Int}\vdash\mathit{x}+\mathit{x}:^{(1,\mathbf{L})}\mathbf{Int}

\displaystyle\mathit{x}:^{(\omega,\mathbf{L})}\mathbf{Int},\mathit{y}:^{(1,\mathbf{M})}\mathbf{Bool}\vdash\mathbf{if}\>\mathit{y}\>\mathbf{then}\>\mathit{x}+\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{else}\>\mathit{x}:^{(1,\mathbf{M})}\mathbf{Int}.

Observe that if we change the assumption
\mathit{x}:^{(\omega,\mathbf{L})}\mathbf{Int}
to
\mathit{x}:^{(1,\mathbf{L})}\mathbf{Int}
or to
\mathit{x}:^{(\omega,\mathbf{H})}\mathbf{Int}
in either judgment, then that judgment would no longer hold. Similarly, if we change the assumption
\mathit{y}:^{(1,\mathbf{M})}\mathbf{Bool}
to
\mathit{y}:^{(1,\mathbf{H})}\mathbf{Bool}
in the second judgment, then too it would no longer hold.

7.
LDC vs. Standard Linear and Dependency Calculi

7.1.
Comparison with Simply-Typed Calculi

In section
3.5
, we discussed how Sealing Calculus, a standard dependency calculus, embeds into LDC(
\mathcal{L}
). Now, we compare LDC with a standard linear calculus, the Linear Nonlinear (LNL)
\lambda
-calculus of
Benton 1994
. The LNL
\lambda
-calculus tracks just linear and unrestricted usage and is simply-typed. So we compare it with simply-typed LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
). Below, we present a meaning-preserving translation from LNL
\lambda
-calculus to LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
).

The LNL calculus employs two forms of contexts and two forms of typing judgments. The two forms of contexts, linear and nonlinear, correspond to assumptions at grades
1
and
\omega
respectively in LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
). The two forms of judgments, linear and nonlinear, correspond to derivations in worlds
1
and
\omega
respectively in LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
). In LNL
\lambda
-calculus, linear and nonlinear contexts are denoted by
\Gamma
and
\Theta
respectively while linear and nonlinear judgments are written as
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{L}}\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}
and
\Theta\vdash_{\mathcal{C}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}
respectively. The calculus contains standard intuitionistic types and linear types; it also contains two type constructors,
F
and
G
, via which the linear and the nonlinear worlds interact. The calculus uses
\mathit{A},\mathit{B}
for linear types;
\mathit{X},\mathit{Y}
for nonlinear types;
\mathit{a},\mathit{b}
for linear variables;
\mathit{x},\mathit{y}
for nonlinear variables;
\mathit{e},\mathit{f}
for linear terms; and
\mathit{s},\mathit{t}
for nonlinear terms.

We present the translation function from LNL
\lambda
-calculus
(
Benton 1994
)
to LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
) in Figure
10
. This translation preserves typing and meaning.

\displaystyle\overline{1}=\mathbf{Unit}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{X}\>\times\>\mathit{Y}}={}^{\omega}\!\overline{\mathit{X}}\>\times\>\overline{\mathit{Y}}

\displaystyle\overline{I}=\mathbf{Unit}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{A}\otimes\mathit{B}}={}^{1}\!\overline{\mathit{A}}\>\times\>\overline{\mathit{B}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{X}\to\mathit{Y}}={}^{\omega}\!\overline{\mathit{X}}\to\overline{\mathit{Y}}

\displaystyle\overline{G\>\mathit{A}}=\overline{\mathit{A}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}}={}^{1}\!\overline{\mathit{A}}\to\overline{\mathit{B}}

\displaystyle\overline{F\>\mathit{X}}={}^{\omega}\!\overline{\mathit{X}}\>\times\>\mathbf{Unit}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{x}}=\mathit{x}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{a}}=\mathit{a}

\displaystyle\overline{()}=\mathbf{unit}

\displaystyle\overline{\ast}=\mathbf{unit}

\displaystyle\overline{(\mathit{s},\mathit{t})}=(\overline{\mathit{s}}^{\omega},\overline{\mathit{t}})

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{e}\otimes\mathit{f}}=(\overline{\mathit{e}}^{1},\overline{\mathit{f}})

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{fst}(\mathit{s})}=\mathbf{let}_{1}\>(\mathit{x}^{\omega},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\overline{\mathit{s}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{x}\hskip 4.0pt(x,y\;\text{fresh})

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{let}\>\mathit{a}\otimes\mathit{b}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{e}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{f}}=\mathbf{let}_{1}\>(\mathit{a}^{1},\mathit{b})\>\mathbf{be}\>\overline{\mathit{e}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\overline{\mathit{f}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{snd}(\mathit{s})}=\mathbf{let}_{1}\>(\mathit{x}^{\omega},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\overline{\mathit{s}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{y}\hskip 4.0pt(x,y\;\text{fresh})

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{let}\>\ast\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{e}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{f}}=\mathbf{let}_{1}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\overline{\mathit{e}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\overline{\mathit{f}}

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}.\mathit{s}}=\lambda^{\omega}\mathit{x}:\overline{\mathit{X}}.\overline{\mathit{s}}

\displaystyle\overline{\lambda\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{e}}=\lambda^{1}\mathit{a}:\overline{\mathit{A}}.\overline{\mathit{e}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{s}\>\mathit{t}}=\overline{\mathit{s}}\>\overline{\mathit{t}}^{\omega}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathit{e}\>\mathit{f}}=\overline{\mathit{e}}\>\overline{\mathit{f}}^{1}

\displaystyle\overline{G\>\mathit{e}}=\overline{\mathit{e}}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{derelict}\>\mathit{s}}=\overline{\mathit{s}}

\displaystyle\overline{F\>\mathit{s}}=(\overline{\mathit{s}}^{\omega},\mathbf{unit})

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{let}\>F\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{e}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{f}}=\mathbf{let}_{1}\>(\mathit{x}^{\omega},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\overline{\mathit{e}}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathbf{let}_{1}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{y}\>\mathbf{in}\>\overline{\mathit{f}}

\displaystyle\hskip 180.0pt(y\;\text{fresh})

Figure 10
.
Type and term translation from LNL
\lambda
-calculus to LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
)

Theorem 7.1.

The translation from LNL
\lambda
-calculus to LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
), shown in Figure
10
, is sound:

•

If
\Theta;\Gamma\vdash_{\mathcal{L}}\mathit{e}:\mathit{A}
, then
\overline{\Theta}^{\omega},\overline{\Gamma}^{1}\vdash\overline{\mathit{e}}:^{1}\overline{\mathit{A}}
.

•

If
\Theta\vdash_{\mathcal{C}}\mathit{t}:\mathit{X}
, then
\overline{\Theta}^{\omega}\vdash\overline{\mathit{t}}:^{\omega}\overline{\mathit{X}}
.

•

If
\mathit{e}=_{\beta}\mathit{f}
, then
\overline{\mathit{e}}=_{\beta}\overline{\mathit{f}}
. If
\mathit{s}=_{\beta}\mathit{t}
then
\overline{\mathit{s}}=_{\beta}\overline{\mathit{t}}
.

Here,
\overline{\Gamma}^{1}
and
\overline{\Theta}^{\omega}
denote
\Gamma
and
\Theta
, with the types translated, and assumptions held at grades
1
and
\omega
respectively. Further,
\_=_{\beta}\_
denotes the beta equivalence relation on the terms of LNL
\lambda
-calculus
(
Benton 1994
)
.

The soundness theorem of LDC, in conjunction with the above meaning-preserving translation, shows that LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
) is no less expressive than the LNL
\lambda
-calculus. In fact, LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
) is more expressive than the LNL
\lambda
-calculus because the latter does not model
0
-usage. Owing to this reason, a translation in the other direction from LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\text{Lin}}
) to LNL
\lambda
-calculus fails!

7.2.
Comparison with Dependently-Typed Calculi

Next, we compare LDC with standard dependently-typed linear and dependency calculi. As discussed in Section
5.1
, there are several calculi for linearity analysis in dependent type systems. Among these calculi, LDC is closest to
GraD
of
Choudhury et al. 2021
. So we compare LDC with
GraD
.

GraD
is a general coeffect calculus parametrized by an arbitrary partially-ordered semiring. LDC(
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
), on the other hand, is a linearity calculus parametrized by specific preordered semirings, i.e.
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
s. When compared over these semirings, we can show that LDC subsumes
GraD
.

Theorem 7.2.

With
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbb{N}}^{\omega}
as the parametrizing structure, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\>\mathit{A}
in
GraD
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}\mathit{A}
in LDC. Further, if
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
in
GraD
, then
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
in LDC.

The above theorem is not surprising because we followed
GraD
while designing LDC.

Next, we compare LDC with standard dependent dependency calculi. In literature, there are only a few calculi
(
Prost 2000
;
Bernardy and
Guilhem 2013
;
Choudhury
et al. 2022
)
on this topic. Among them, the calculus
\text{DDC}^{\top}

(
Choudhury
et al. 2022
)
inspired the design of LDC, as discussed in Section
2.4
. Owing to this reason, LDC is similar to
\text{DDC}^{\top}
, as far as dependency analysis is concerned. Formally, we can show that when parametrized over arbitrary lattices, LDC subsumes
\text{DDC}^{\top}
.

Theorem 7.3.

With
\mathcal{L}
as the parametrizing structure, if
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
in
\text{DDC}^{\top}
, then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\ell}\mathit{A}
in LDC. Further, if
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
in
\text{DDC}^{\top}
, then
\vdash\mathit{a}\leadsto\mathit{a^{\prime}}
in LDC.

7.3.
Comparison with Other Related Calculi

In this paper, we presented a unified perspective on usage and dependency analyses. There is a precedent to our presentation:
Benton and Wadler 1996
observed that models of linear logic also provide models of Moggi’s computational metalanguage. Now, broadly speaking, usage analysis may be seen as fine-grained linear logic in action and dependency analysis as fine-grained computational metalanguage in action. Therefore, our unified perspective on usage and dependency analyses may be seen as a generalization of the observation made by
Benton and Wadler 1996
.

Next, linearity and dependency have been traditionally analyzed as a coeffect and an effect respectively. There is existing work
(
Gaboardi et al. 2016
)
in literature on combining effects and coeffects.
Gaboardi et al. 2016
present a calculus that employs distinct graded modalities for analyzing coeffects and effects, which are then combined by allowing the modalities to interact via graded distributive laws. In contrast, LDC employs the same graded modality for analyzing both linearity and dependency but draws the grades from different algebraic structures during the two analyses. Further, the graded modality employed in LDC is both monadic and comonadic whereas the graded modalities employed in
Gaboardi et al. 2016
are either monadic or comonadic but not necessarily both. The main reason behind these differences is that while the motivation of
Gaboardi et al. 2016
is a general calculus for combining coeffects and effects, our motivation is a specialized calculus for combining linearity and dependency. Owing to this specialized nature, our calculus does not need multiple modalities or graded distributive laws. Finally, note that the calculus presented in
Gaboardi et al. 2016
is simply-typed whereas LDC allows dependent types.

8.
Conclusion

We have shown that linearity and dependency analyses can be systematically unified and combined into a single calculus. We presented, LDC, a general calculus for combined linearity and dependency analysis in pure type systems. We showed that linearity and dependency analyses in LDC are sound using a heap semantics. We also showed that LDC subsumes standard calculi for linearity and dependency analyses. In this paper, we focused on the syntactic properties of LDC. In a future work, we plan to explore the semantic properties of the calculus. In particular, we want to find out how semantic models of LDC compare with the categorical models of linear and dependency type systems.

Appendix A
Join Not Derivable in Graded-Context Type Systems

Proposition A.1.

Graded-context type systems
(
Ghica and Smith 2014
;
Brunel
et al. 2014
;
Petricek
et al. 2014
;
Atkey 2018
;
Orchard
et al. 2019
;
Abel and Bernardy 2020
;
Choudhury et al. 2021
)
cannot derive a monadic join operator.

Proof.

Graded-context type systems mentioned in the proposition vary slightly in their design. However, all of them contain a core graded calculus. Below, we first present this Core Graded Calculus,
GCore
, and thereafter show that
GCore
cannot derive a monadic join operator.

GCore
is parametrized by an arbitrary preordered semiring
\mathcal{Q}=(Q,+,\cdot,0,1<:)
. If parametrized by
\mathcal{Q}
, the calculus is referred to as
GCore
(
\mathcal{Q}
). The grammar and typing rules of
GCore
(
\mathcal{Q}
) appear in Figures
11
and
12
respectively. The operations on contexts are defined as in Section
3.1
.

\displaystyle\text{grades},q\in Q

\displaystyle::=0\>|\>1\>|\>\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>|\>\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\>|\>\ldots

\displaystyle\text{types},A,B

\displaystyle::=\mathbf{Bool}\>|\>\mathit{A}\multimap\mathit{B}\>|\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}

\displaystyle\text{terms},a,b

\displaystyle::=x\>|\>\lambda\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}\>|\>\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}\>|\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{a}\>|\>\mathbf{let}\>!_{\mathit{q}}\>\mathit{x}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}

\displaystyle|\>\mathbf{true}\>|\>\mathbf{false}\>|\>\mathbf{if}\>\mathit{b}\>\mathbf{then}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathbf{else}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\text{contexts},\Gamma

\displaystyle::=\emptyset\>|\>\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}

.

\mathcal{Q}
)

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:\mathit{A}

(Typing Rules)

}{},}\mpr@andcr\frac{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:\mathbf{Bool}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{A}\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}\quad\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rfloor=\lfloor\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rfloor}{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{if}\>\mathit{b}\>\mathbf{then}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\mathbf{else}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{A}}\quad\textsc{narrower=0.3,lab={\hypertarget{ottalt:rule:ott:GC-If}{{{GC-If}}}

}{},}

.
Typing rules for GC(
\mathcal{Q}
)

Now, towards contradiction, assume that
GCore
(
\mathcal{Q}
) can derive a monadic join operator. Then, for any
\mathit{A}
, there exists a closed non-constant function of type
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}\to\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathit{A}
for all
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\in\mathcal{Q}
. By model-theoretic arguments, we shall show that for some
\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathit{A}
,
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, any function having such a type must be constant.

Fix
\mathcal{Q}
to be the following preordered semiring. The underlying set is
\{0,1,k_{1},k_{2}\}
and the semiring operations and preorder are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq k_{2}\text{ if }\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\notin\{0\}

\displaystyle\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq k_{2}\text{ if }\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\notin\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}<:\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

Check that the above definitions satisfy the axioms for preordered semirings. Now, note that
\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\cdot\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
. Below, we shall show that there exists no non-constant function of type
\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{Bool}\to\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{Bool}
in
GCore
(
\mathcal{Q}
).

Towards contradiction, suppose such a function,
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{Bool}\to\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{Bool}
exists. Then,
\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{true})
and
\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{false})
would reduce to different values (assume a call-by-value reduction). Without loss of generality, say
\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{true})\leadsto^{\ast}\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{true}
and
\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{false})\leadsto^{\ast}\>\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{false}
, where
\leadsto^{\ast}
is the multistep call-by-value reduction relation. Now, for any sound interpretation,
\llbracket\_\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}
, of
GCore
(
\mathcal{Q}
) in a model
\mathcal{M}
, we should have:

\llbracket\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{true})\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}=\llbracket\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{true}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}\text{ and }\llbracket\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{false})\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}=\llbracket\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{false}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}

We construct a sound model of
GCore
(
\mathcal{Q}
) where these equalities lead to a contradiction.

Let
\mathbf{Set}
be the category of sets and functions. Note that
\mathbf{Set}
is a symmetric monoidal category with the monoidal product given by cartesian product. Now, any
\mathcal{Q}
-graded linear exponential comonad on
\mathbf{Set}
provides a sound interpretation of
GCore
(
\mathcal{Q}
)
(
Katsumata 2018
)
. We define
\mathbf{!}
, a
\mathcal{Q}
-graded linear exponential comonad on
\mathbf{Set}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{!}(0)=\mathbf{!}(k_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\ast}

\displaystyle\mathbf{!}(1)=\mathbf{!}(k_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Id}

Here,
\mathbf{Id}
is the identity functor and
\mathbf{\ast}
is the functor that maps every object to the terminal object. The morphisms associated with
\mathbf{!}
are as expected.

Now, interpreting using
\mathbf{!}
, we have:

\llbracket\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{true})\rrbracket_{(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{!})}=\llbracket\mathit{f}\>(\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{false})\rrbracket_{(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{!})}=\text{app}\>\circ\langle\llbracket\mathit{f}\rrbracket_{(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{!})},\langle\rangle\rangle

But
\llbracket\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{true}\rrbracket_{(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{!})}\neq\llbracket\mspace{2mu}!_{\mathit{k_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\mspace{1mu}\mathbf{false}\rrbracket_{(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{!})}
. A contradiction.

∎

Appendix B
Linearity Analysis in Simply-Typed LDC

Lemma B.1 (Multiplication (Lemma
3.1
)).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
, then
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

•

Rule
ST-Var
. Have:
0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A},0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Need to show:
0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A},0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

This case follows by rule
ST-Var
.

•

Rule
ST-Lam
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r})}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

By associativity of multiplication,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r})=(\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q})\cdot\mathit{r}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-Lam
.

•

Rule
ST-App
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r})}\mathit{A}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-App
, using associativity and distributivity properties.

•

Rule
ST-Pair
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r})}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-Pair
, using associativity and distributivity properties.

•

Rule
ST-LetPair
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}})}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r})}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}})}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-LetPair
, using associativity and distributivity properties.

•

Rule
ST-Unit
. Have:
0\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{unit}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathbf{Unit}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(0\cdot\Gamma)\vdash\mathbf{unit}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathbf{Unit}
.

This case follows by rule
ST-Unit
, using associativity and identity properties.

•

Rule
ST-LetUnit
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathbf{Unit}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}})}\mathbf{Unit}
and
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-LetUnit
, using associativity and distributivity properties.

•

Rule
ST-Inj1
. Have
\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{inj}_{1}\>\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-Inj1
.

•

Rule
ST-Inj2
. Similar to rule
ST-Inj1
.

•

Rule
ST-Case
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{case}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash\mathbf{case}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{of}\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>;\>\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}})}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}+\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}})}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
and
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}})}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-Case
, using associativity and distributivity properties.

•

Rule
ST-SubL
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
where
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma<:\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Next,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma<:\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
, since
\Gamma<:\Gamma^{\prime}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-SubL
.

•

Rule
ST-SubR
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q^{\prime}}}\mathit{A}
where
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
\mathit{q}<:\mathit{q^{\prime}}
.

Need to show:
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q^{\prime}}}\mathit{A}
.

By IH,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Next,
\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q}<:\mathit{r_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{q^{\prime}}
, since
\mathit{q}<:\mathit{q^{\prime}}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-SubR
.

∎

Lemma B.2 (Factorization (Lemma
3.2
)).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
and
q\neq 0
, then there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

•

Rule
ST-Var
. Have:
0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A},0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A}
.

Need to show:
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{x}:^{1}\mathit{A}
and
0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A},0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

This case follows by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{1}\mathit{A},0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

•

Rule
ST-Lam
. Have:
\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma,\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
.

Need to show:
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\lambda^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{x}:\mathit{A}.\mathit{b}:^{1}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

By IH,
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

Since
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
q\neq 0
, therefore
\mathit{r}<:\mathit{r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

By rule
ST-SubL
,
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
.

This case, then, follows by rule
ST-Lam
by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

•

Rule
ST-App
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}
.

Need to show:
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}:^{1}\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

There are two cases to consider.

–

r\neq 0
. Since
q\neq 0
, therefore
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}\neq 0
.

By IH,
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{1}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}\mathit{A}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r})\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

By Lemma
B.1
,
\mathit{r}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A}
.

By rule
ST-App
,
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\mathit{r}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{\mathit{r}}:^{1}\mathit{B}
.

This case, then, follows by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\mathit{r}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

–

r=0
. Then,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{0}\mathit{A}
. By Lemma
B.1
,
0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{0}\mathit{A}
.

By IH,
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{1}{}^{0}\!\mathit{A}\to\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

By rule
ST-App
,
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{b}\>\mathit{a}^{0}:^{1}\mathit{B}
.

Further,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
(
\because

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}<:\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
for any
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}
in
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
and
\overline{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}}=\overline{0}
in
\mathbb{N}_{=}
).

This case, then, follows by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

•

Rule
ST-Pair
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{\mathit{q}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

Need to show:
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{1}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

There are two cases to consider.

–

r\neq 0
. Since
q\neq 0
, therefore
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r}\neq 0
.

By IH,
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{1}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{1}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}<:(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r})\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

By Lemma
B.1
,
\mathit{r}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

By rule
ST-Pair
,
\mathit{r}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{1}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

This case, then, follows by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\mathit{r}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

–

r=0
. Then,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{0}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
. By Lemma
B.1
,
0\cdot\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:^{0}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

By IH,
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:^{1}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

By rule
ST-Pair
,
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{1}}}}^{0},\mathit{a_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):^{1}{}^{0}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

Further,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
(
\because

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
for any
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}
in
\mathbb{N}_{\geq}
and
\overline{\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\overline{0}
in
\mathbb{N}_{=}
).

This case, then, follows by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

•

Rule
ST-LetPair
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1
.

Need to show:
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Since
q\neq 0
and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1
, therefore
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\neq 0
.

By IH,
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
\mathit{r^{\prime}},\mathit{q^{\prime}}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{r^{\prime}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\mathit{q^{\prime}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}<:(\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}})\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{r^{\prime}}
and
\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q^{\prime}}
.

Since
q\neq 0
, therefore
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}<:\mathit{r^{\prime}}
and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:\mathit{q^{\prime}}
.

By rule
ST-SubL
,
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathit{x}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\mathit{r}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{y}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
.

Again, by Lemma
B.1
,
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}{}^{\mathit{r}}\!\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\>\times\>\mathit{A_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

By rule
ST-LetPair
,
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>(\mathit{x}^{\mathit{r}},\mathit{y})\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
.

This case, then, follows by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

•

Rule
ST-Unit
. Have:
0\cdot\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{unit}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathbf{Unit}
.

Need to show:
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathbf{unit}:^{1}\mathbf{Unit}
and
0\cdot\Gamma<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

This case follows by setting
\Gamma^{\prime}:=0\cdot\Gamma
.

•

Rule
ST-LetUnit
. Have:
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{\mathit{q}\cdot\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\mathbf{Unit}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{\mathit{q}}\mathit{B}
and
\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}<:1
.

Need to show:
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mathbf{let}_{\mathit{q_{{\mathrm{0}}}}}\>\mathbf{unit}\>\mathbf{be}\>\mathit{a}\>\mathbf{in}\>\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}<:\mathit{q}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}
.

By IH,
\exists\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathit{a}:^{1}\mathbf{Unit}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathit{b}:^{1}\mathit{B}
and
